\chapter*{Abstract}

\arch{} is a RISC architecture designed with simplicity as its dominating
principle. it separates instructions from constants, uses a compact and
regular variable-length instruction format, and encodes scalar control
flow with a single-bit predicate register that corresponds to vector
mask operations. the design places particular emphasis on the relationship
between instruction encoding, execution, virtualization, memory management,
capabilities, and isolation. rather than treating these concerns separately,
the architecture integrates them into a coherent model that is both
practical to implement and straightforward to understand.

\vspace{10em}

\chapter*{Preface}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface}

this document introduces a RISC architecture designed with simplicity
as its dominating principle, along with several features not yet present
in mainstream RISC architectures:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
  \item a compression technique for split instruction and constant streams,
        keeping instruction words uniform by relocating constants to a
        secondary constant stream.
  \item a variable-length instruction format designed for vectorized decoding,
        that uses a fixed-position size field to efficiently resolve
        instruction boundaries in parallel.
  \item an efficient frontier between hardware synthesis and software
        translation, achieved by a super-regular encoding scheme that lowers
        instruction decode complexity.
  \item a modern approach to virtualization, memory management, and
        capabilities, that supports context-switched virtual domains and
        page-table color permissions.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the document begins with a general introduction to RISC architectures,
including principal elements such as load-store operation, memory,
registers, instructions, and constants. it then presents an overview of
the proposed architecture, followed by sections on program streams, the
variable-length instruction format, the register file, type formats,
the memory model, the instruction set, the assembly syntax, and the
programming language calling convention.
\para
this document does not simply present an architecture reimplementing
existing ideas; rather, it introduces a novel architecture featuring an
instruction coding scheme optimized for efficient parallel decoding in
hardware or software. novel too are its contemporary instruction semantics,
which use single-bit predicates for scalar compare, divide, branch, and
arithmetic with carry, to achieve a correspondence with vector operations
using mask registers.
\para
in addition to the core instruction set, the architecture also defines
extensions for capabilities, virtual domains, software-defined memory
management and environment. the capability extension adds fine-grained
page-based permissions and role based access control. the domain
extension adds virtualization for trap routing, page translation,
message-signaled interrupt delivery, and context switching. the machine
extension provides for custom address translation schemes. finally,
the environment extension adds controls for power and reset.
\para
this work is intended for computer architects, systems programmers,
students of computing machinery, and anyone interested in the evolution
of RISC design. by the end of the document, readers should be able to
reason about computer programs at the instruction level and understand
the logic behind the architectural design choices—with enough detail
to implement the architecture in hardware or software.

\chapter{Architecture}
\pagenumbering{arabic}

\section{Introduction}

this section gives a brief introduction to RISC architectures.
\para
a RISC\footnote{Reduced Instruction Set Computer}%
~\cite{cocke1990risc,patterson-hennessy,patterson1980risc} machine is a type of
general-purpose computer with the characteristic that it has a reduced
set of instructions in contrast to a CISC\footnote{Complex Instruction
Set Computer} machine. A RISC machine is Turing complete meaning it can
perform any computation that a Turing machine can, given enough time and
memory. a Turing machine~\cite{Turing1936} is a theoretical model of
computation.
\para
a RISC machine has a set of instructions which comprise basic operations
such as: \emph{load-from-memory}, \emph{store-to-memory}, \emph{add},
\emph{subtract}, \emph{compare}, plus \emph{conditional branch} and
\emph{unconditional branch} instructions et cetera; which one can imagine
as a list of instructions on a paper tape. each instruction has an
\emph{opcode}, which is a unique binary pattern that identifies the
\emph{operation}, plus several \emph{operands}, which are arguments
to the instruction.

\begin{Verbatim}
    register-0 = load-from-memory at tape-address-0
    register-1 = load-from-memory at tape-address-1
    register-2 = add register-0 and register-1
                 store-to-memory register-2 at tape-address-2 
\end{Verbatim}

\par\noindent
some instructions have operands that point to values inside \emph{registers}
in a \emph{register-file}, which is like a nearby filing cabinet containing
cards with numbers on them. some of these values are addresses that point
to values in \emph{main-memory}, which is like a larger but slower and more
distant filing cabinet. some of them are \emph{immediate} values, which are
small numbers listed inside of the instructions on the paper tape.
\para
the paper tape is just a way conceptualize a list of instructions stored
in \emph{main-memory}. there is a special register called \emph{PC} short
for \emph{program counter}, which points to the current position on the
tape. after each instruction executes the tape is advanced to the next
instruction and the \emph{program counter} is incremented, until it
encounters a \emph{branch} instruction which causes it to move forwards
or backwards to a different position on the tape. branch instructions can
be \emph{conditional} or \emph{unconditional}. conditional branches are
selectively executed based on the results of a comparison instruction.

\newpage
\subsection{load-store}

a \emph{load-store} architecture~\cite{cocke1990risc} is a way to
characterize RISC architectures where most instructions have simple operands
that point to values held in registers, with \emph{load} and \emph{store}
memory access instructions to retrieve and commit values to main memory.
a \emph{load-store} architecture alleviates the need for complex addressing
modes and dedicated \emph{input/output} instructions.
\emph{input/output} to peripherals and secondary storage use \emph{MMIO}%
\footnote{\emph{MMIO}\,-- Memory-mapped I/O.}.

\subsection{registers}

registers are temporary storage used to fill input and output
operands for the \emph{ALU}\footnote{\emph{ALU}\,-- Arithmetic logic unit.}
before and after execution of instructions. registers are
organized as a word-addressable store where each register number refers
to data of width specified by the \emph{GLEN} parameter
\S\ref{sec:architectural-parameters}.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]

\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \fill [black!5] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \draw [black] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\z}{int(3-\x)}
  \node [font=\footnotesize,anchor=base] at (4,\x+0.25) {$\mathsf{register\;\z}$};
}

\draw [black,dashed] (0,-1)      rectangle (8,0);

\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \draw [black] (8,\x)    rectangle (24,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \foreach \y in {0,4,...,60} {
    \fill [black!5]  (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
    \draw [black!45] (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
  }
  \foreach \y in {2,6,...,62} {
    \fill [black!9] (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
    \draw [black!45] (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
  }
  \draw [black] (8,\x) rectangle (24,\x+1);
}
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (8,4.2)  {\textsf{GLEN}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (24,4.2)  {\textsf{0}};

\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}

\label{fig:register-organization}
\end{figure}

\subsection{memory}

main memory is primary storage which in modern computers is most likely
\emph{DRAM}\footnote{\emph{DRAM}\,-- Dynamic random-access memory.}.
main memory is organized as a byte-addressable store where each address
refers to a byte which is 8-bits of data, with the address width specified
by the \emph{ALEN} parameter \S\ref{sec:architectural-parameters}.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]

\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \fill [black!5] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \draw [black] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\z}{int((3-\x)*8)}
  \node [font=\footnotesize,anchor=base] at (4,\x+0.25) {$\mathsf{address\;\z}$};
}
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,4.2)  {\textsf{ALEN}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (8,4.2)  {\textsf{0}};

\draw [black,dashed] (0,-1)      rectangle (8,0);

\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \foreach \y in {0,2,...,6} {
    \fill [black!5] (8+\y*2,\x)     rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
    \draw [black] (8+\y*2,\x)    rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
  }
  \foreach \y in {1,3,...,7} {
    \fill [black!9] (8+\y*2,\x)    rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
    \draw [black] (8+\y*2,\x)    rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
  }
}
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (9,4.2)  {\textsf{byte-7}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (23,4.2)  {\textsf{byte-0}};

\draw [black,dashed] (8,-1)      rectangle (24,0);

\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{organization of main-memory storage.}
\label{fig:main-memory-organization}
\end{figure}

\newpage
\subsection{instructions}

instruction memory is a memory region dedicated to program instructions.
it is organized as a word-addressable store, similar to main memory,
but is read-only and reserved exclusively for instructions. this is
conventionally referred to as a Harvard architecture~\cite{aiken1946marki},
in contrast to a Von Neumann architecture~\cite{vonneumann1945edvac},
which uses shared memory for instructions and data.
\para
instruction words contain packets of 16-bits which are composed of
size, opcode, and operand fields. instruction packets can be appended
together to form larger instruction words with larger opcode and
operand fields.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\node at (15.5,1.3) [font=\tiny] {\textsf{1}};
\node at (16.5,1.3) [font=\tiny] {\textsf{0}};
\node at (10.5,1.3) [font=\tiny] {\textsf{6}};
\node at (14.5,1.3) [font=\tiny] {\textsf{2}};
\node at (1.5,1.3) [font=\tiny] {\textsf{15}};
\node at (9.5,1.3) [font=\tiny] {\textsf{7}};

\draw (10.0,1.0) -- (10.0,1.5);
\draw (1.0,1.0) -- (1.0,1.5);

\par\noindent
instruction words are packed together into instruction blocks
containing instruction size information that is used to fuse
them together to compose variable-length instructions~\cite{US5717881A}.
instruction words can begin on any 16-bit aligned address.
instruction memory is addressed with displacements from the \emph{program
counter} called \emph{pc-relative} addresses.
\\

\newpage
\subsection{constants}

constant memory is a memory region dedicated to constants. it is organized
as a word-addressable store like main-memory, but is read-only and
restricted to constants. instructions can refer to constants with
\emph{immediate-base-register-relative} addresses in instruction operands.
\para
constant memory addresses are stored in instruction operand slots as
displacements from the \emph{immediate base} register and are referred to
as \emph{ib-relative} addresses. \emph{ib-relative} addresses are sized,
scaled, and aligned relative to the \emph{immediate
base} register, which anchors the current immediate block.
\emph{ib-relative} addresses alias a single constant storage space.
these diagrams show $ib8(n)$ through to $ib64(n)$ addresses for access
to 8-bit through to 64-bit constants.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]
\foreach \x in {0,...,1} {
  \fill [black!5] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);

\section{Pipeline model}

an illustrative micro-architecture is shown based on the classic 5-stage
RISC pipeline~\cite{thornton1970cdc6600,patterson-hennessy} with the addition of an
\emph{operand fetch} stage and a \emph{constant memory} port. this revised
6-stage micro-architecture is composed of the following pipeline stages:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{symbol} & \thead{stage} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{IF} & \textsf{instruction fetch}  & reads instructions from memory into a fetch buffer. \\
\texttt{ID} & \textsf{instruction decode} & decodes instruction length, opcode, and operands. \\
\texttt{OF} & \textsf{operand fetch}      & reads operands from register file and constant memory. \\
\texttt{EX} & \textsf{execute}            & performs logical operations or arithmetic on the operands. \\
\texttt{MA} & \textsf{memory access}      & loads data from or stores data to memory. \\
\texttt{WB} & \textsf{writeback}          & writes results back to the register file. \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph 6-stage example pipeline.}
\label{tab:glyph-example-pipeline}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
a simplified micro-architecture using those pipeline stages might look like
the following. this example omits hazard detection and forwarding logic
for the sake of simplicity.
a more complete micro-architecture may have branch predictors, instruction
and constant stream prefetchers, instruction and data caches, translation
lookaside buffers, parallel instruction decoders, register renamers, reorder
buffers, scheduler queues, and load and store buffers.
\\

]

\node[stage] (IF) {\textsf{IF} -- instruction fetch};

\node[stage, below=of ID] (OF) {\textsf{OF} -- operand fetch};

\newpage
\section{Program streams}

\arch{} separates the stored program into two streams, one with instructions
and one with constants~\cite{Minsky1967,ti1983tms32010,analog1994adsp21000},
analogous to the use of separate tapes for program and constants in a
multi-tape universal Turing machine model.
the instruction stream is addressed using the \emph{program counter (pc)} and
the constant stream is addressed using the \emph{immediate base (ib)} register.
this architecture is referred to as a split program stream Harvard architecture.

\label{fig:glyph-program-streams}
\end{figure}

\par\noindent
instruction blocks are aligned memory blocks addressed by the \emph{program
counter}. instruction memory is addressed with \emph{pc-relative} addresses
in instruction immediate values or indirectly with constants accessed using
\emph{ib-relative} addresses stored inside instruction operand slots.
\para
immediate blocks are aligned memory blocks addressed by the \emph{immediate
base} register. constant memory is addressed with \emph{ib-relative}
addresses stored inside instruction operand slots, which are sized, scaled,
and aligned relative to the \emph{immediate base} register.
\para
the constant stream can branch independently, using the
\emph{immediate-block-jump} instruction to update the \emph{immediate base}
register, or together with the \emph{program counter} during procedure calls
and returns.
\emph{jump-and-link-immediate-block} and \emph{jump-to-link-immediate-block}
add and subtract address vectors to \emph{(pc,ib)}. the \emph{pack-indirect}
instruction allows absolute \emph{(pc,ib)} addresses to be packed into an
address vector for indirect calls.
\para
the instruction forms use bonded operand slots for immediate operands and
operand bits do not overlap opcode bits. the use of immediate blocks means
large immediate constants can all be accessed using short references encoded
inside of operand slots for instructions that use an immediate block
relative addressing mode.
\\

\newpage
\section{Design overview}

\par\noindent
\arch{} is a super regular RISC architecture that encodes constants in a
secondary stream accessed via an \emph{immediate base} that points at
immediate block constants using a constant address mode.
the \emph{immediate base} register branches like the \emph{program counter},
and procedure calls and returns set and restore \emph{(pc,ib)} together.
\para
the architecture uses relative address vectors to pack two \emph{(pc,ib)}
displacements into a single link register. this avoids the need for a
second link register to store the immediate base, maintaining compatibility
with the single link register on conventional RISC architectures.
\para
immediate blocks can be switched using the immediate block branch instruction.
updates to the immediate base register via instructions labeled as branches
signal to micro-architects that a constant stream prefetcher and branch
predictor are expected.
unlike conventional RISC architectures~\cite{patterson1981risc,hennessy1981mips,waterman2016riscv},
which synthesize large constants inline
using long-form instruction sequences, immediate block constants use C-style
structure packing and alignment. this keeps relocations word-aligned
and avoids patching instructions.
\para
this list outlines differentiating features of the architecture:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item variable length instruction format supporting 16, 32, and 64-bit
instructions.
\item 1-bit predicate for compare, branch, divide-by-zero, add or subtract
with carry.
\item \emph{(pc,ib)} is a \emph{program counter} and
\emph{immediate base} register address vector.
\item link register contains a packed relative \emph{(pc,ib)} address vector
to function entry.
\item \texttt{ibj} --- \emph{immediate-block-jump} adds a relative address
to the \emph{immediate base} register.
\item \texttt{mov} --- \emph{move-word} uses a displacement to access an
\emph{immediate block constant}.
\item \texttt{jal} --- \emph{jump-and-link} or \emph{link-function-call}
copies the address vector into the link register and adds \emph{immediate
block constants} to \emph{(pc,ib)}.
\item \texttt{jtl} --- \emph{jump-to-link} or \emph{link-function-return}
subtracts link vector from \emph{(pc,ib)} and adds \emph{immediate block
constants}.
\item \texttt{pin} --- \emph{pack-indirect} packs two absolute addresses
as a relative address vector from \emph{(pc,ib)} and is used for calling
absolute addresses such as virtual functions.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the \texttt{pin} and \texttt{link} instructions form a modular arithmetic
ring of relative address vectors. calling a function adds a relative
address vector to \emph{(pc,ib)} and stores it in the link register.
returning subtracts the link vector and adds the block entry constants,
restoring \emph{(pc,ib)} to the call site. tail calls are the additive
identity of this ring, and they replace \emph{(pc,ib)} without storing a
link, composing directly with the caller's link register.

\newpage
\section{Design parameters}

\par\noindent
\arch{} has several architectural parameters that define the dimensions of an
instance of the architecture.
configurations of these parameters are grouped into architectural profiles.

\subsection{architectural parameters}
\label{sec:architectural-parameters}

the architecture provides a unified floating-point and integer general
register file with 8 or 64 registers, and a unified floating-point
and integer vector register file with 64 registers.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 16-bit instruction packet can access 8 registers with up to 3 operands.
\item 32-bit instruction packet can access 64 registers with up to 3 operands.
\item 64-bit instruction packet can access 64 registers with up to 6 operands.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the architectural parameters are as follows:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Parameter} & \thead{Value}& \thead{Description} \\
\hline
GREG & 8, 64      & number of general registers \\
VREG & --, 64     & number of vector registers \\
NDOM & --, 16     & number of domain contexts \\
NCOL & --, 16     & number of architectural colors \\
MLEN & 32         & width of message addresses in bits \\
ILEN & 16, 32, 64 & width of largest instruction in bits \\
ALEN & 64         & width of arithmetic lane in bits \\
GLEN & 64, 128    & width of general registers in bits \\
VLEN & 512, 4096  & width of vector registers in bits \\
SLEN & 128        & width of vector subgroup in bits \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{architectural parameters}
\label{tab:architectural-parameters}
\end{table}

\vspace{-1em}

\subsection{architectural profiles}

the architectural parameters are linked to the dimensions of the
register slots in the variable length instruction packets. the 16-bit
packet can be used in a freestanding micro-controller profile. the 32-bit
packet contains scalar and packed-SIMD\footnote{SIMD - Single Instruction
Multiple Data} instructions on general registers. the 64-bit packet
contains packed-SIMD instruction on vector registers.

\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
{\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}

\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{profile} &
\thead{GREG} & \thead{VREG} & \thead{NDOM} & \thead{NCOL} & \thead{MLEN} &
\thead{ILEN} & \thead{ALEN} & \thead{GLEN} & \thead{VLEN} & \thead{SLEN} \\
\hline
\emph{general-min} & 8  & -  & -  & -  & 32  & 16 & 64  & 64 & -    & -   \\
\emph{general-max} & 64 & -  & 16 & 16 & 32  & 32 & 64  & 128 & -    & -   \\
\emph{vector-min} & 64 & 64 & 16 & 16 & 32  & 64 & 64  & 128 & 512  & 128 \\
\emph{vector-max} & 64 & 64 & 16 & 16 & 32  & 64 & 64  & 128 & 4096 & 128 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
}

\label{tab:architectural-profiles}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Instruction format}

\arch{} instructions use a variable length format~\cite{US5717881A} with
a 16-bit instruction packet that supports 16, 32, 64, and 128-bit
instruction words. the instruction packet uses a \emph{super regular}
scheme, whereby successive instruction packets extend the fields in
the previous instruction packet~\cite{US6877084B1}. instruction words
can begin on any 16-bit aligned address.


\subsection{instruction sizes}

the variable length instruction format has a \emph{2-bit} size field
inspired by LEB128~\cite{leb128} in a fixed position in every instruction
packet, to reduce the complexity of size-decoding for variable length
instructions\footnote{instructions are considered \emph{well-formed}
if the size field of subsequent packets has the value \texttt{11}.}.
the following table shows the size fields for the set of instruction sizes:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|c|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Instruction size} & \thead{Size vector} \\
\hline
16-bit   & \texttt{\{00}\}                       \\
32-bit   & \texttt{\{01,11\}}                    \\
64-bit   & \texttt{\{10,11,11,11\}}              \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{variable-length instruction size fields}
\label{tab:instruction-size-fields}
\end{table}

\subsection{instruction decoding}

the instruction size encoding and field extension scheme has been designed
to minimize complexity for parallel decoding in hardware and software.
the following table shows the instruction width combinations for
various width parallel instruction decoders:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Packets} & \thead{Bits} & \thead{Bytes} & $\bm{n_{combos}}$ & $\bm{\log_2(n)}$ \\
\hline
1-wide  & 16-bit   & 2  & 1     & \fmtlogtwo{1} \\
2-wide  & 32-bit   & 4  & 16    & \fmtlogtwo{16} \\
4-wide  & 64-bit   & 8  & 58    & \fmtlogtwo{58} \\
8-wide  & 128-bit  & 16 & 574   & \fmtlogtwo{574} \\
16-wide & 256-bit  & 32 & 51904 & \fmtlogtwo{51904} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{instruction decode combinations}
\label{tab:instruction-decode-combos}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{instruction forms}

the variable length instruction format has a single base form where
fields in the template are extended by successive instruction packets.
the instructions forms are super regular in that operand and opcode
bits do not overlap and the number and complexity of the formats is
reduced so that vectorized instruction decoding is simple in both
hardware and software.

this section details the layout of the variable sized instructions:

% instruction fields

\newcommand{\fieldcell}[5]{%
  \fill[black!5] (#2,\py)rectangle(#3,{\py+1});
  \draw[black!90](#2,\py)rectangle(#3,{\py+1});
  \node at(#4,{\py+0.5})[font=\footnotesize]{$\mathsf{#1_{#5}}$};
}

\newcommand{\formfieldsI}  [6]{\fieldcell{#3}{1}{10}{5.5}{#6}}
\newcommand{\formfieldsRI} [6]{\fieldcell{#3}{4}{10}{7}  {#6}
                               \fieldcell{#2}{1}{4} {2.5}{#5}}
\newcommand{\formfieldsRRI}[6]{\fieldcell{#3}{7}{10}{8.5}{#6}
                               \fieldcell{#2}{4}{7} {5.5}{#5}
                               \fieldcell{#1}{1}{4} {2.5}{#4}}

\subsubsection{\isize-bit instruction forms}

this section details the layout of the \isize-bit instruction forms:

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{minipage}[c]{1.0\textwidth}

\foreach \fname/\blA/\blB/\blC/\boA/\boB/\boC/\cAn/\cBn/\cCn/\cDn/\cEn/\cFn/\fdesc
  [count=\fcount from 1]
  in {
  {op\isize{}\fmta} /0/0/9/0/0/0/{}  /{}  /{imm}/{}  /{}  /{imm}/{\isize-bit large immediate},
  {op\isize{}\fmtb} /0/3/6/0/2/0/{}  /{rc}/{imm}/{}  /{rf}/{imm}/{\isize-bit \cntb{} operand with immediate},
  {op\isize{}\fmtc} /3/3/3/2/2/0/{rc}/{rb}/{imm}/{rf}/{re}/{imm}/{\isize-bit \cntc{} operand with immediate},
  {op\isize{}\fmtd} /3/3/3/2/2/2/{rc}/{rb}/{ra} /{rf}/{re}/{rd} /{\isize-bit \cntd{} operand}
} {

\noindent
\begin{minipage}[c]{0.53\linewidth}
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\ifcase\fcount \or \hdri \or \hdrri \or \hdrrr \or \hdrrr \fi
\foreach \p in {0,...,\numexpr\packets-1\relax} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\py}   {-\p}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\oplo} {int(5*\p)}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\ophi} {int(5*\p+4)}
  \ifnum\p=0 \edef\szlit{\szfirst} \else \edef\szlit{11} \fi
  % sz field
  \fill[black!5] (15,\py)rectangle(17,{\py+1});
  \draw[black!90](15,\py)rectangle(17,{\py+1});
  \node at(16,{\py+0.5})[font=\scriptsize]{$\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\szlit}}$};
  % opcode field
  \fill[black!5] (10,\py)rectangle(15,{\py+1});
  \draw[black!90](10,\py)rectangle(15,{\py+1});
  \node at(12.5,{\py+0.5})[font=\footnotesize]{$\mathsf{opcode_{[\ophi:\oplo]}}$};
  % operand fields: dispatched by form
  \ifnum\p<2
    \def\nA{\cAn}\def\nB{\cBn}\def\nC{\cCn}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\rlo}{int(\blA*\p)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\rhi}{int(\blA*(\p+1)-1)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\slo}{int(\blB*\p)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\shi}{int(\blB*(\p+1)-1)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\tlo}{int(\blC*\p)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\thi}{int(\blC*(\p+1)-1)}
  \else
    \def\nA{\cDn}\def\nB{\cEn}\def\nC{\cFn}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\rlo}{int(\blA*(\p-\boA))}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\rhi}{int(\blA*(\p-\boA+1)-1)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\slo}{int(\blB*(\p-\boB))}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\shi}{int(\blB*(\p-\boB+1)-1)}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\tlo}{int(\blC*(\p-\boC))}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\thi}{int(\blC*(\p-\boC+1)-1)}
  \fi
  \edef\brA{[\rhi:\rlo]}\edef\brB{[\shi:\slo]}\edef\brC{[\thi:\tlo]}
  \ifcase\fcount
    \or \formfieldsI  {\nA}{\nB}{\nC}{\brA}{\brB}{\brC}
    \or \formfieldsRI {\nA}{\nB}{\nC}{\brA}{\brB}{\brC}
    \or \formfieldsRRI{\nA}{\nB}{\nC}{\brA}{\brB}{\brC}
    \or \formfieldsRRI{\nA}{\nB}{\nC}{\brA}{\brB}{\brC}
  \fi
}
\end{tikzpicture}

\section{Register file}

\newcommand{\registerfile}[8]{
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\zoffset}{#1}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\zwidth}{#2}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\zreg}{#3}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\zdiv}{#4}
  \def\zpre{#5}
  \def\zlast{#6}
  \def\zlen{#7}
  \def\zlabel{#8}

  \foreach \z in {0,...,7} {
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\za}{int((\z==6)*(\zreg>8))}
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\zb}{int((\z==7)*(\zreg>8))}
    \ifnum\za=1\relax
      \node[black!50, font=\large] at (16-\zwidth/2,\zoffset-\z+0.7) {$\vdots$};
    \else
      \fill [black!5] (16-\zwidth,\zoffset-\z+1)    rectangle (16,\zoffset-\z);
      \draw [black] (16-\zwidth,\zoffset-\z+1)    rectangle (16,\zoffset-\z);
      \ifnum\zdiv>0\relax
        \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\zmax}{\zdiv-1}
        \foreach \x in {1,...,\zmax} {
          \pgfmathsetmacro{\xo}{16-\zwidth+\x*\zwidth/\zdiv}
          \draw[black!20] (\xo,\zoffset-\z+1) -- (\xo,\zoffset-\z);
        }
      \fi
      \ifnum\zb=1\relax
        \node [font=\scriptsize, anchor=base]
           at (16-\zwidth/2,\zoffset-\z+0.25) {\textsf{\zpre\zlast}};
      \else
        \node [font=\scriptsize, anchor=base]
           at (16-\zwidth/2,\zoffset-\z+0.25) {\textsf{\zpre\z}};
      \fi
    \fi
  }

  \node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west]
     at (16-\zwidth,\zoffset+1.2) {\textsf{\zlen}};
  \node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east]
     at (16,\zoffset+1.2)  {\textsf{0}};

  \draw [decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt,mirror,raise=2mm}]
        (16,\zoffset-7) -- (16,\zoffset+1)
        node[midway,xshift=3mm,align=center,font=\scriptsize,
             text width=17mm,anchor=west]{\textsf{\shortstack{\zlabel}}};
}

\newcommand{\assignpair}[3]{%
  \expandafter\assignpairaux#3\relax{#1}{#2}%
}

\def\assignpairaux#1,#2\relax#3#4{%
  \expandafter\def\csname#3\endcsname{#1}%
  \expandafter\def\csname#4\endcsname{#2}%
}

\newcommand{\registerstate}[7]{%
  \begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]

  \assignpair{alen}{awidth}{#1}
  \assignpair{xlen}{xwidth}{#2}
  \assignpair{vlen}{vwidth}{#3}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\aw}{\awidth}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\xw}{\xwidth}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\vw}{\vwidth}
  \assignpair{xreg}{xlast}{#4}
  \assignpair{vreg}{vlast}{#5}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\xr}{\xreg}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\vr}{\vreg}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\xdiv}{#6}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\vdiv}{#7}

  \registerfile{7}{\xw}{\xr}{\xdiv}{r}{\xlast}{\xlen}{general\\registers}

  \ifnum\vr>0\relax
    \registerfile{-2}{\vw}{\vr}{\vdiv}{v}{\vlast}{\vlen}{vector\\registers}
  \fi

  \node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (16-\aw,11.2)  {\textsf{\alen}};
  \node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (16,11.2)  {\textsf{0}};

  \fill [black!15] (12,9)    rectangle (16,10);
  \draw [black]    (12,9)    rectangle (16,10);

  \fill [black!15] (12,10)    rectangle (16,11);
  \draw [black]    (12,10)    rectangle (16,11);

  \fill [black!15] (14,12)    rectangle (16,13);
  \draw [black]    (14,12)    rectangle (16,13);

  \node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (14,9.25)  {\textsf{ib}};
  \node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (14,10.25)  {\textsf{pc}};
  \node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (15,12.25)  {\textsf{flag}};

  \draw [decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt,mirror,raise=2mm}]
      (16,12) -- (16,13) node[midway,xshift=3mm,align=center,font=\scriptsize,
          text width=17mm,anchor=west]{\textsf{\shortstack{predicate}}};

  \draw [decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt,mirror,raise=2mm}]
      (16,9) -- (16,11) node[midway,xshift=3mm,align=center,font=\scriptsize,
          text width=17mm,anchor=west]{\textsf{\shortstack{control\\registers}}};

\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
}

% register specifications

\newcommand{\registerspecrow}[4]{%
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\scl}{#2/#1}%
  \edef\xinit{#2}%
  \edef\yinit{#3}%
  \draw [black] (0,\yinit+0)    rectangle (\xinit,\yinit+1);
  \foreach \bl/\rlt/\mlt/\llt/\lm
    [remember=\xl as \xr (initially \xinit)]
    in {#4} {
    \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\isneg}{\bl<0 ? 1 : 0}%
    \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\bl}{abs(\bl)}%
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\xl}{\xr - \bl*\scl}%
    \pgfmathsetmacro{\xm}{(\xr+\xl)/2}%
    \ifnum\isneg=1
      \foreach \b in {0,...,\numexpr\bl-1\relax} {
        \pgfmathsetmacro{\bxl}{\xr - (\b+1)*\scl}%
        \pgfmathsetmacro{\bxr}{\xr - \b*\scl}%
        \draw[black!25,dashed] (\bxl,\yinit+0) rectangle (\bxr,\yinit+1);
      }
    \fi
    \draw[black] (\xl,\yinit+0) rectangle (\xr,\yinit+1);
    \ifx\rlt\empty\else
      \node[font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (\xr,\yinit+1.25) {\textsf{\rlt}};
    \fi
    \ifx\mlt\empty\else
      \node[font=\tiny,anchor=base]      at (\xm,\yinit+1.25) {\textsf{\mlt}};
    \fi
    \ifx\llt\empty\else
      \node[font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (\xl,\yinit+1.25) {\textsf{\llt}};
    \fi
    \ifx\lm\empty\else
      \node[font=\small,anchor=base]     at (\xm,\yinit+0.25) {\lm};
    \fi
  }
}
\newcommand{\registerspec}[3]{%
  \par\noindent%
  \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
  \registerspecrow{#1}{#2}{0.0}{#3}
  \end{tikzpicture}%
}

the architectural register state comprises a predicate register, a program
counter, an immediate base register, a unified floating-point and integer
general register file, and an optional unified floating-point and integer
vector register file.
SIMD instructions are supported on both the general port and the vector port.
general registers support narrow SIMD for address generation and control flow.
vector registers support wide SIMD parallelism for throughput.

\subsection{template configuration}

the register state accessible by the template architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item ALEN-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item ALEN-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item GREG \texttimes\,GLEN-bit general registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r\textit{N}}).
\item VREG \texttimes\,VLEN-bit vector registers (\textsf{v0} --- \textsf{v\textit{N}}).
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following diagram shows the template configuration
register state:
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\registerstate{ALEN,4}{GLEN,8}{VLEN,16}{64,\textit{N}}{64,\textit{N}}{0}{0}
\caption{template configuration register state.}
\label{fig:glyph-register-state-template}
\end{figure}

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{general-min} configuration}

the register state accessible by the \emph{general-min} architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item 64-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item 64-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item 8 \texttimes\,64-bit general registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r7}).
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following table shows the \emph{general-min} configuration
register storage:
\\
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{type} & \thead{count} & \thead{width} & \thead{size (bits)} & \thead{size (bytes)} \\
\hline
control & 2 & 64 & 128 & 16 \\
general & 8 & 64 & 512 & 64 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{\emph{general-min} configuration register storage.}
\label{tab:general-min-configuration-register-storage}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the following diagram shows the \emph{general-min} configuration
register state:
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\registerstate{64,4}{64,4}{VLEN,0}{8,7}{0,0}{0}{0}
\caption{\emph{general-min} configuration register state.}
\label{fig:glyph-register-state-general-min}
\end{figure}

\par\noindent
when $\mathit{GLEN} < \mathit{ALEN} \times 2$ it is not possible to pack
absolute addresses in the link register, so the \texttt{PIN} and
\texttt{LINK} instructions save relative addresses in the link register.
this increases latency in branch address calculation compared to
absolute addresses.

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{general-max} configuration}

the register state accessible by the \emph{general-max} architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item 64-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item 64-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item 64 \texttimes\,128-bit general registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r63}) with \emph{SIMD128}.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following table shows the \emph{general-max} configuration
register storage:
\\
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{type} & \thead{count} & \thead{width} & \thead{size (bits)} & \thead{size (bytes)} \\
\hline
control & 2  & 64  & 128  & 16 \\
general & 64 & 128 & 8192 & 1024 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{\emph{general-max} configuration register storage.}
\label{tab:general-max-configuration-register-storage}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the following diagram shows the \emph{general-max} configuration
register state:
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\registerstate{64,4}{128,8}{0,0}{64,63}{0,0}{0}{0}
\caption{\emph{general-max} configuration register state.}
\label{fig:glyph-register-state-general-max}
\end{figure}

\par\noindent
when $\mathit{GLEN} \ge \mathit{ALEN} \times 2$ it is possible to pack
absolute addresses in the link register, so the \texttt{PIN} and
\texttt{LINK} instructions save absolute addresses in the link register.
this reduces latency in branch address calculation compared to
relative addresses.

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{vector-min} configuration}

the register state accessible by the \emph{vector-min} architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item 64-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item 64-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item 64 \texttimes\,128-bit general registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r63}) with \emph{SIMD128}.
\item 64 \texttimes\,512-bit vector registers (\textsf{v0} --- \textsf{v63}) with \emph{SIMD512}.
\end{itemize}

\end{figure}

\newpage
\section{Type formats}

the architecture uses three basic scalar types with the scalar and packed SIMD instructions:

\vspace{-0.3em}

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item unsigned fixed-width integer
\item signed fixed-width two's complement integer
\item fixed-width binary floating-point number
\end{itemize}

\subsection{numeric rules}

this section describes the numeric encoding, arithmetic, and comparison
rules for signed and unsigned integers and for floating-point numbers.

\subsubsection{unsigned fixed-width integer rules}

unsigned fixed-width integers are represented using an \emph{n}-bit
form, with range $[0,\; 2^{n}-1]$. arithmetic operations on unsigned
integers wrap around on overflow according to modulo $2^n$ arithmetic.
relational comparisons are performed according to unsigned magnitude.

\subsubsection{signed fixed-width integer rules}

signed fixed-width integers are represented using an \emph{n}-bit
two’s complement form, with range $[-2^{n-1},\; 2^{n-1}-1]$.
negative numbers are encoded by inverting all bits and adding one.
addition and subtraction operations on signed integers wrap
around on overflow according to modulo $2^n$ arithmetic.
multiplication and division operations on signed integers truncate
toward zero and set the sign to the \emph{exclusive-or} of the two signs.
relational comparisons are performed according to signed magnitude.

\subsubsection{binary floating-point number rules}

binary floating‑point number formats and arithmetic rules conform to the
IEEE standard for floating‑point arithmetic, \emph{IEEE-754-2019}~%
\cite{ieee754-2019}, which specifies fixed‑width binary interchange formats,
encoding of sign, exponent, and fraction fields, and arithmetic, comparison,
and rounding behavior, including the handling of exceptional values such
as NaNs and infinities.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item the quiet NaN has the most significant bit of the fraction field set to one.
\item the signaling NaN has the most significant bit of the fraction field set to zero.
\item the canonical NaNs have the sign bit and all remaining payload bits set to zero.
\item all other NaN payloads are reserved for the implementation.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the 8‑bit binary floating‑point formats follow the definitions in the
current IEEE P754 working draft, and the 16‑bit brain floating‑point
format \emph{bfloat16} is specified by the \emph{OpenCL bfloat16
Conversions Extension}.

\section{Type conventions}

the architecture provides a common type naming convention for
integer and floating-point scalar and packed SIMD vector types.
each type is described by several attributes:

\vspace{-0.3em}

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item unique type name
\item unique short symbol used in opcodes
\item vector length (omitted for scalar types)
\item mnemonic indicating the operand type and width
\item human-readable description
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
short symbols for signed integers have single-letter codes. unsigned types
and floating-point symbols are explicitly distinguished by \texttt{u} and
\texttt{f} suffixes, eliminating ambiguity with single-letter symbols.
the mnemonics have been chosen to disambiguate operand size and type.

the following table lists scalar types, their symbols, mnemonics, and descriptions:

\texttt{i16}  & \texttt{w}  & \emph{word}   & 16-bit signed integer (word) \\
\texttt{i32}  & \texttt{d}  & \emph{dword}  & 32-bit signed integer (double-word) \\
\texttt{i64}  & \texttt{q}  & \emph{qword}  & 64-bit signed integer (quad-word) \\

\newpage
\section{Memory model}

the memory model covers address spaces, isolation,
memory classes, types, and ordering rules.
address spaces define isolation and memory class.
memory types define caching and write behavior.
atomic operations define synchronization and ordering.

\subsection{address spaces}

address space prefixes support architectural role checking based on address.
address spaces extend a concept known as \emph{linear address space separation}%
~\cite{intel-sdm}, originally derived from the Linux kernel convention,
whereby the upper half of the address space is reserved for system software,
and the invariant on the address role is checked during execution.
\para
the following unary prefixes identify each address space and its characteristics:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{prefix} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{0b0.} & user & virtual memory accessible to unprivileged software with the \emph{user} role. \\
\texttt{0b10.} & system & virtual memory accessible to privileged software with the \emph{system} role. \\
\texttt{0b110.} & physical & globally visible memory with well-defined cache-coherency and ordering. \\
\texttt{0b1110.} & device & memory mapped I/O for peripherals and special-purpose devices. \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph address spaces.}
\label{tab:glyph-address-spaces}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
\textbf{user: }
user memory is a virtual address space intended for application
software executing with the \emph{user} role.
user memory is isolated from the \emph{system} address space except
through controlled privilege transitions.
user memory may map physical memory, device memory, or other
implementation-defined memory classes.
\para
\textbf{system: }
system memory is a virtual address space intended for operating systems
and other privileged software executing with the \emph{system} role.
system memory is isolated from the \emph{user} address space except
through controlled privilege transitions.
system memory may map physical memory, device memory, or other
implementation-defined memory classes.
\para
\textbf{physical: }
physical memory is a memory class with well-defined visibility,
cache-coherency, and memory-ordering semantics.
physical memory provides a view of random-access memory shared
by all processing units in the system.
physical memory may be accessed directly by privileged software or
mapped into the \emph{user} or \emph{system} virtual address spaces.
\para
\textbf{device: }
device memory is a memory class with non-uniform access semantics,
intended for memory-mapped I/O and special purposes such as high-speed
scratchpad memory.
device memory can be mapped into the \emph{user} or \emph{system}
virtual address spaces.
device memory is not required to be coherent or globally ordered
and is typically uncached.

\subsection{cache coherency}

the architecture supports coherent caching of memory regions using
memory types for cache policy.
memory types define cache coherency attributes for caching and
write propagation behavior.
memory types are assigned via memory type registers or address translation
page table attributes to determine how memory regions interact with the
cache hierarchy.
\para
memory regions that have a memory type with caching enabled must support
misaligned loads and stores, excluding instructions that are explicitly
defined to require alignment.
\para
the following table lists memory types, their symbols, and descriptions:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{symbol} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{0b00} & write-back    & \emph{WB} & cache reads and defer writes until eviction or explicit flush. \\
\texttt{0b01} & write-combine & \emph{WC} & cache and coalesce writes within cache lines before writeback. \\
\texttt{0b10} & write-through & \emph{WT} & cache reads with all writes forwarded through to memory. \\
\texttt{0b11} & uncached      & \emph{UC} & all accesses bypass the cache hierarchy. \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph memory types.}
\label{tab:glyph-memory-types}
\end{table}

\subsection{memory ordering}

the architecture provides a memory consistency model%
~\cite{lamport1979multiprocessor,sewell2010x86tso,pulte2018armv8}
designed for
practical implementation in hardware or for translation to processors
with \emph{weakly-ordered} \emph{acquire-release} memory models and to
processors with \emph{strongly-ordered} memory models that possess a
\emph{total store order}.
the memory consistency model has the following properties:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item memory writes have a total store order.
\item memory writes become visible to other threads at synchronization points.
\item memory reads cannot be reordered across synchronization points.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the design supports translation to instruction sets with
weakly-ordered memory operations.
to achieve this, synchronized operations must enforce a total order among
themselves, realized at synchronization points immediately following them.
a synchronized operation is any operation that performs a globally
ordered write such as an atomic read-modify-write operation.
the synchronization point acts as an implicit write ordering barrier.
on weakly-ordered systems, a synchronizing memory ordering barrier is
inserted at the synchronization point to prevent subsequent reads from
being reordered with respect to it.
\para
thus, weakly-ordered systems with acquire-release ordering require
synchronizing memory barriers after atomic read-modify-write
operations to act as implicit synchronization points.
loads observe ordering established by prior synchronization points.
on architectures providing sufficient ordering guarantees, loads may be
implemented as relaxed operations.

\addtocontents{toc}{\protect\clearpage}
\chapter{System registers}

this chapter describes the user-level, system, capability, domain,
machine, and environment registers. the registers are used to manage
user-level context, trap handling, address translation, interrupt
routing, capabilities, virtual domains, machine, and environment.
\para
reserved and unlabeled register fields will read as zero and ignore writes.
registers narrower than the general register width will return zero in upper
bits and ignore writes to upper bits. read-write fields of all registers,
if otherwise unspecified, are initialized to zero after reset.
\para
the user-level and privileged system registers are organized into the following categories:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|X|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{2}{|l|}{\tsub{core}} \\
\hline
\textbf{user-level registers}  & floating-point status and control,
time stamp counter and frequency, thread and domain address aliases. \\
\textbf{privileged registers}   & trap handling, clock, thread addresses,
interrupts, address translation, inter-domain messages and debugging. \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{2}{|l|}{\tsub{extension}} \\
\hline
\textbf{capability extension}  & page-based roles and permissions using page table colors. \\
\textbf{domain extension}      & domain addresses, trap routing, and system context vectors. \\
\textbf{machine extension}     & machine features, addresses, and translation fault handling. \\
\textbf{environment extension} & environment diagnostics, reset, and power. \\
\textbf{array extension}       & array unit and instructions for tiled array fetches. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{system registers}
\label{tab:system-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{User-level registers}
the architecture provides user-level registers for floating-point
status and control, time stamp counter and frequency, and machine and
domain address aliases.
\para
user-level registers can be read and written using the \texttt{sysread},
\texttt{syswrite}, \texttt{sysset}, and \texttt{sysclear} instructions.
user-level registers are available with user-level privileges when their
associated bit is enabled in \texttt{scontrol}, otherwise they require
the system role to access.

\subsubsection{user-level registers}
the following table lists the user-level registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{floating-point unit registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x00} & fpstatus  & floating-point status \\
\texttt{0x01} & fpcontrol & floating-point control \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{time stamp counter registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x02} & stamp     & time stamp counter \\
\texttt{0x03} & freq      & time stamp frequency \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{thread address registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x04} & address   & user machine address \\
\texttt{0x05} & domain    & user domain address \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{user-level registers}
\label{tab:user-level-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{floating-point unit registers}
the section describes the user-level floating-point unit registers.

\subsubsection{floating-point status (\texttt{fpstatus})}
\texttt{fpstatus} is a read-only register containing status
information for floating-point operations. the \texttt{Z, O, U, X,}
and \texttt{I} fields contain accrued floating-point exceptions.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\texttt{I},%
  1//{1}//\texttt{X},%
  1//{2}//\texttt{U},%
  1//{3}//\texttt{O},%
  1//{4}//\texttt{Z}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & I   & invalid operation \\
\verb|1 << 1| & X   & inexact result \\
\verb|1 << 2| & U   & numeric underflow \\
\verb|1 << 3| & O   & numeric overflow \\
\verb|1 << 4| & Z   & divide by zero \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{floating-point status flags}
\label{tab:floating-point-status-flags}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{floating-point control (\texttt{fpcontrol})}
\texttt{fpcontrol} is a read-write register containing control
information for floating-point operations. the \texttt{RM} field
contains the current floating-point rounding mode. the \texttt{Z}
field enables \emph{flush-to-zero} and \emph{denormals-are-zero}.
the \texttt{N} field enables \emph{not-strict-ieee754}, allowing
\emph{fused-multiply-add} and other optimizations that may affect
precision.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\texttt{Z},%
  1//{1}//\texttt{N},%
  2/{2}//{3}/\texttt{RM}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & Z   & \emph{flush-to-zero} and \emph{denormals-are-zero} \\
\verb|1 << 1| & N   & \emph{not-strict-ieee754} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{floating-point control flags}
\label{tab:floating-point-control-flags}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|0| & RN   & round to nearest (even) \\
\verb|1| & RD   & round down (towards $-\infty$) \\
\verb|2| & RU   & round up (towards $+\infty$) \\
\verb|3| & RZ   & round towards zero (truncate) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{floating-point control round mode field}
\label{tab:floating-point-control-round-mode-field}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{time stamp counter registers}
the section describes the user-level time stamp counter and frequency registers.

\subsubsection{time stamp counter (\texttt{stamp})}
\texttt{stamp} is a read-only register containing a high-precision
time stamp counter that increments at the frequency specified by
\texttt{freq}.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.T} is enabled.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//63/\emph{time stamp counter}%
}

\subsubsection{time stamp frequency (\texttt{freq})}
\texttt{freq} is a read-only register containing the frequency of the
time stamp counter, \texttt{stamp}, in Hertz (Hz).
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.T} is enabled.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//63/\emph{time stamp frequency}%
}

\newpage
\subsection{thread address registers}
the section describes the user-level thread address registers.

\subsubsection{user machine address (\texttt{address})}
\texttt{address} is a read-only register containing an alias of the unique
system-wide machine address for this thread from \texttt{saddress}.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.D} is enabled.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{machine address}%
}

\subsubsection{user domain address (\texttt{domain})}
\texttt{domain} is a read-only register containing an alias of the
system-wide domain address for this thread from \texttt{sdomain}.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.D} is enabled.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{domain address}%
}

\newpage
\section{Privileged registers}
the architecture provides privileged system-level registers for
trap handling, clock, thread addresses, interrupts, address translation,
inter-domain messages and debugging.
\para
privileged system-level registers can be read and written
using the \texttt{sysread}, \texttt{syswrite}, \texttt{sysset}, and
\texttt{sysclear} instructions. privileged system-level registers are
accessible with the system role, which requires executable pages in
the system or physical address spaces.

\subsubsection{privileged registers}
the following table lists the privileged trap, system, and debug registers:

\hline
\texttt{0x10} & tstatus   & trap status \\
\texttt{0x11} & tcontrol  & trap control \\
\texttt{0x12} & tscratch  & trap scratch \\
\texttt{0x13} & tcause    & trap cause \\
\texttt{0x14} & tvalue    & trap value \\
\texttt{0x15} & tack      & trap acknowledge \\
\texttt{0x16} & tepc      & trap exception program counter  \\
\texttt{0x17} & teib      & trap exception immediate base \\
\texttt{0x18} & thpc      & trap handler program counter  \\
\texttt{0x19} & thib      & trap handler immediate base \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{privileged system registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x20} & sstatus   & system status \\

\texttt{0x22} & sfeature  & system feature \\
\texttt{0x23} & sversion  & system version \\
\texttt{0x24} & sclock    & system clock \\
\texttt{0x25} & saddress  & system machine address \\
\texttt{0x26} & sdomain   & system domain address \\
\texttt{0x27} & spti      & system page table info \\
\texttt{0x28} & sptr      & system page table root \\
\texttt{0x29} & sie       & system interrupt enable \\
\texttt{0x2A} & sip       & system interrupt pending \\

\newpage
\subsection{privileged trap registers}
the section describes the privileged trap registers.

\subsubsection{trap status (\texttt{tstatus})}
\texttt{tstatus} is a read-only register containing trap status.
the \texttt{T} field indicates whether a timer interrupt is pending.
the \texttt{M} field indicates whether a message interrupt is pending.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\texttt{T},%
  1//{1}//\texttt{M}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & T   & timer interrupt pending \\
\verb|1 << 1| & M   & message interrupt pending \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap status fields}
\label{tab:trap-status-fields}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{trap control (\texttt{tcontrol})}
\texttt{tcontrol} is a read-write register containing control bits. the
\texttt{I} field is read-write and controls whether system interrupts
are enabled.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\texttt{I}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & I   & system interrupt enable \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap control fields}
\label{tab:trap-control-fields}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{trap scratch (\texttt{tscratch})}
\texttt{tscratch} is a read-write save register for use during trap handling.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{GLEN-1}/\emph{scratch value}%
}

\newpage
\subsubsection{trap cause (\texttt{tcause})}
\texttt{tcause} is a read-only register that contains the cause of the
current trap.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  6/{0}//{5}/\emph{cause}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{system exceptions}} \\ \hline
\verb|1| & break-instruction   & break instruction exception \\
\verb|2| & illegal-instruction & illegal instruction exception \\
\verb|3| & debug-monitor       & debug monitor exception \\
\verb|4| & misaligned-fetch    & fetch misaligned \\
\verb|5| & misaligned-load     & load misaligned \\
\verb|6| & misaligned-store    & store misaligned \\
\verb|7| & access-fault-fetch  & fetch access fault \\
\verb|8| & access-fault-load   & load access fault \\
\verb|9| & access-fault-store  & store access fault \\
\verb|10| & page-fault-fetch   & fetch page fault \\
\verb|11| & page-fault-load    & load page fault \\
\verb|12| & page-fault-store   & store page fault \\
\verb|13| & capability-fault   & capability fault \\
\verb|14| & domain-fault       & domain fault \\
\verb|15| & machine-fault      & machine fault \\
\verb|16| & tlb-miss-fault     & TLB miss fault \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{system interrupts}} \\ \hline
\verb|30| & timer-interrupt     & timer interrupt \\
\verb|31| & message-interrupt   & message interrupt \\
\hline
\verb|32...63| & interrupt-n    & interrupt pins 0 -- 31 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap causes}
\label{tab:trap-causes}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{trap value (\texttt{tvalue})}
\texttt{tvalue} is a read-only register containing the identity of the trap
and may contain:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item the faulting instruction word for \emph{break} and \emph{illegal instruction exceptions}.
\item the faulting address for \emph{misaligned}, \emph{access} and \emph{page faults}.
\item the masked thread or domain address for \emph{message interrupts}.
\item the system clock time for \emph{timer interrupts}.
\end{itemize}

\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{GLEN-1}/\emph{trap value}%
}

\newpage
\subsubsection{trap acknowledge (\texttt{tack})}
\texttt{tack} is a write-only register where the trap cause is written
to acknowledge the trap so that interrupts are not delivered
while state is being saved, and for double-faults to be detected.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  6/{0}//{5}/\emph{cause}%
}

\subsubsection{trap exception program counter (\texttt{tepc})}
\texttt{tepc} is a read-write register which contains the \emph{program
counter} address before the trap.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{program counter}%
}

\subsubsection{trap exception immediate base (\texttt{teib})}
\texttt{teib} is a read-write register which contains the \emph{immediate
base} address before the trap.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{immediate base}%
}

\subsubsection{trap handler program counter (\texttt{thpc})}
\texttt{thpc} is a read-write register containing the address of the
system trap handler routine.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{program counter}%
}

\subsubsection{trap handler immediate base (\texttt{thib})}
\texttt{thib} is a read-write register containing the address of the
system trap handler constants.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{immediate base}%
}

\newpage
\subsection{privileged system registers}
the section describes the privileged system registers.

\subsubsection{system status (\texttt{sstatus})}
\texttt{sstatus} is a read-only register containing system status bits.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%

}

\newpage
\subsubsection{system version (\texttt{sversion})}
\texttt{sversion} is a read-only register containing a specification
version number described by its \emph{major}, \emph{minor}, and \emph{patch}
fields. the allowable values are specified in the following table.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{7}/\emph{patch},%
  8/{8}//{15}/\emph{minor},%
  8/{16}//{23}/\emph{major}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{major} & \thead{minor} & \thead{patch} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|0| & \verb|7| & \verb|0| & pre-release current \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{system version values}
\label{tab:system-version-values}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{system clock (\texttt{sclock})}
\texttt{sclock} is a read-only register containing the wall clock time as a
signed integer representing elapsed nanoseconds (ns) relative to the J2000
epoch (\texttt{2000-01-01T12:00:00Z}), informally noon on 1 January 2000 UTC.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.T} is enabled.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//63/\emph{wall clock time}%
}

\subsubsection{system machine address (\texttt{saddress})}
\texttt{saddress} is a read-only register containing the unique system-wide
machine address for this thread. the system-wide machine
address is used as a target for inter-domain messages.
this address must not alias across processors.
this address is switched during domain context switching.
the machine address prefix should be unary \texttt{0001}.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{machine address}%
}

\subsubsection{system domain address (\texttt{sdomain})}
\texttt{sdomain} is a read-write register containing the system-wide
domain address for this thread. the system-wide domain address
is used as a target for inter-domain messages.
this address may alias across processors.
this address is switched during domain context switching.
the domain address prefix should be unary \texttt{1110}.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{domain address}%
}

\newpage
\subsubsection{system page table info (\texttt{spti})}
\texttt{spti} is a read-only register containing page table info
such as supported page table formats.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 2| & \texttt{vm12d} & 4-level page tables with 4~KiB leaf pages \\
\verb|1 << 4| & \texttt{vm14c} & 3-level page tables with 16~KiB leaf pages \\
\verb|1 << 8| & \texttt{vm16c} & 3-level page tables with 64~KiB leaf pages \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{system page table info bits}
\label{tab:system-page-table-info-bits}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{system page table root (\texttt{sptr})}
\texttt{sptr} is a read-write register containing the page table root
pointer for address translation.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{63}/\emph{page table root}%
}

\subsubsection{system interrupt enable (\texttt{sie})}
\texttt{sie} is a read-write register that contains interrupt enable flags
for 32 wired interrupt pins.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  32/{0}//{31}/\emph{interrupt enable 0-31}%
}

\subsubsection{system interrupt pending (\texttt{sip})}
\texttt{sip} is a read-only register that contains interrupt pending flags
for 32 wired interrupt pins.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  32/{0}//{31}/\emph{interrupt pending 0-31}%
}

\subsubsection{system deadline timer (\texttt{stimer})}
\texttt{stimer} is a read-write register containing the one-shot time stamp
deadline for this thread. when the time stamp counter, \texttt{stamp}, is
greater than or equal to the time stamp deadline, a \emph{timer interrupt}
is generated, the \texttt{T} field of the \texttt{tstatus} register is set
to pending, and the \texttt{stimer} register is reset to zero. a value of
zero indicates the one-shot timer is disabled.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//63/\emph{time stamp deadline}%
}

\newpage
\subsubsection{system target address (\texttt{starget})}
\texttt{starget} is a read-write register containing the system-wide
target address of a set of threads to send message interrupts to.
this register must be set before writing to \texttt{smessage}.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item all-zeros is the \emph{boot processor}.
\item all-ones is the \emph{broadcast address}.
\item unary prefix \texttt{0001?} should be a \emph{machine address}.
\item unary prefix \texttt{1110?} should be a \emph{domain address}.
\end{itemize}

\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{target address}%
}

\subsubsection{system target mask (\texttt{smask})}
\texttt{smask} is a read-write register containing the system-wide
address mask of a set of threads to send message interrupts to.
this register must be set before writing to \texttt{smessage}.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{target mask}%
}

\subsubsection{system target message (\texttt{smessage})}
\texttt{smessage} is a write-only register that causes an edge-triggered
\emph{message interrupt} to be queued to the set of threads specified in
the \texttt{starget} and \texttt{smask} registers. message interrupts are
marked pending in the \texttt{M} field of the \texttt{tstatus} register
of the receiving threads. message interrupt queuing can fail due to
\emph{head-of-line blocking} or other transport faults. \texttt{sstatus.E}
is set if the most recent message was discarded due to an exception.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{message signal}%
}

\newpage
\subsection{privileged debug registers}
the section describes the privileged debug registers.

\subsubsection{debug status (\texttt{dstatus})}
\texttt{dstatus} is a read-only register containing status bits for debug
monitor exceptions.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\texttt{S},%
  1//{1}//\texttt{F},%
  1//{2}//\texttt{R},%
  1//{3}//\texttt{W}%
}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & S   & stopping instruction \\
\verb|1 << 1| & F   & fetch monitor address \\
\verb|1 << 2| & R   & load monitor address \\
\verb|1 << 3| & W   & store monitor address \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{debug status fields}
\label{tab:debug-status-fields}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{debug cycle counter (\texttt{dcycle})}
\texttt{dcycle} is a read-only register containing the number of cycles
retired since power on.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{cycle counter}%
}

\subsubsection{debug instruction counter (\texttt{dinst})}
\texttt{dinst} is a read-only register containing the number of instructions
retired since power on.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{instruction counter}%
}

\subsubsection{debug stop instruction (\texttt{dstop})}
\texttt{dstop} is a read-write register containing an instruction number
to halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug monitor exception}.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{stopping instruction}%
}

\newpage
\subsubsection{debug monitor fetch address (\texttt{dfetch})}
\texttt{dfetch} is a read-write register containing a memory fetch address
to monitor for and halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug monitor
exception} for a matching memory fetch address.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{fetch address}%
}

\subsubsection{debug monitor read address (\texttt{dread})}
\texttt{dread} is a read-write register containing the memory load
address to monitor for and halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug
monitor exception} for a matching memory load address.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{read address}%
}

\subsubsection{debug monitor write address (\texttt{dwrite})}
\texttt{dwrite} is a read-write register containing the memory store
address to monitor for and halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug
monitor exception} for a matching memory store address.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{write address}%
}

\section{Capability extension}

the architecture provides an optional capability extension that provides
a capability-based security model which includes capability role exceptions
for capability violations routed through the domain extension, role-based
access control, and page-based permissions using page table colors.
the capability extension integrates with the domain and machine extensions
to form the architectural isolation model.
color permissions mask page table permissions based on page table color.
\iffalse
color roles restrict access to privileged registers and instructions based on
page table color.
\fi
privileged registers and instructions have capability roles associated with them.
capability role exceptions are routed using the domain extension.
\para
the architecture is designed to work without the capability extension.
if the capability extension is not present, the domain, capability,
machine, and message roles map to the system role, and the user and
system roles are derived from their respective address space prefixes.
the capability and domain extensions are detected and enabled via the machine extension.
\para
the following table lists the privileged capability system parameters:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabularx}{95mm}{|l|X|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{description} & \thead{default} \\
\hline
$\mathit{ncol}$      & the number of architectural colors & 16 \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{capability parameters}
\label{tab:capability-parameters}
\end{table}

\subsection{capability permission registers}

this section describes the \emph{capability permission registers} which
define \emph{page table color} permission masks for page table entries.
when \texttt{capcontrol.P} and \texttt{mcontrol.C} are set, the
\emph{capability permission registers} are combined with page table
permissions to limit maximum \emph{read} and \emph{write} permission
based on page table color.

\subsubsection{color read permission (\texttt{colorread})}
\texttt{colorread} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing read permissions for \emph{color-x},
used to check loads from pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{read perms}%
}

\subsubsection{color write permission (\texttt{colorwrite})}
\texttt{colorwrite} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing write permissions for \emph{color-x},
used to check stores to pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{write perms}%
}

\iffalse
\subsubsection{color exec permission (\texttt{colorexec})}
\texttt{colorexec} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing execute permissions for \emph{color-x},
used to check execution in pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{exec perms}%
}
\fi

\iffalse
\newpage
\subsection{capability role registers}

this section describes the \emph{capability role registers}.
the \emph{capability role registers} control access to privileged
registers and instructions based on \emph{page table color} of the
executable text.
when \texttt{capcontrol.R} and \texttt{mcontrol.C} are set, privileged
registers and instructions cause capability traps for illegal accesses,
which are restricted based on the \emph{system}, \emph{domain},
\emph{capability}, and \emph{machine} roles.

\subsubsection{color system role (\texttt{colorsys})}
\texttt{colorsys} is a read-write register containing an array of
\textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing system role for
\emph{color-x}, used to check access to privileged trap
registers, privileged system registers and privileged debug registers
from executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{system role}%
}

\subsubsection{color domain role (\texttt{colordom})}
\texttt{colordom} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing domain role for \emph{color-x}
that are used to check access to privileged domain registers from
executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{domain role}%
}

\subsubsection{color capability role (\texttt{colorcap})}
\texttt{colorcap} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing capability role for \emph{color-x}
that are used to check access to privileged capability registers from
executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{capability role}%
}

\subsubsection{color machine role (\texttt{colormac})}
\texttt{colormac} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing machine role for \emph{color-x}
that are used to check access to privileged machine registers from
executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{machine role}%
}
\fi

\iffalse
\newpage
\subsection{capability matrix registers}

this section describes the color matrix registers. the \emph{capability
matrix registers} are permission registers that protect access to
memory based on a combination of \emph{source} and \emph{destination}
page table color and \emph{read}, or \emph{write} permissions.
when \texttt{capcontrol.M} is set, memory accesses to pages with matching
\emph{source} and \emph{destination} page table colors, and wider
permissions, will cause capability traps.

\subsubsection{color matrix read permissions (\texttt{colormatread}$[\mathit{ncol}]$)}
\texttt{colormatread}$[\mathit{ncol}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ncol}} read-write
registers containing an array of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits with read
permissions, used to check loads from executable pages with a
matching register \emph{page table color} to memory addresses in
memory pages with a matching \emph{page table color} index.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{read perms}%
}

\subsubsection{color matrix write permissions (\texttt{colormatwrite}$[\mathit{ncol}]$)}
\texttt{colormatwrite}$[\mathit{ncol}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ncol}} read-write
registers containing an array of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits with write
permissions, used to check stores from executable pages with a
matching register \emph{page table color} to memory addresses in
memory pages with a matching \emph{page table color} index.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{write perms}%
}

\subsubsection{color matrix exec permissions (\texttt{colormatexec}$[\mathit{ncol}]$)}
\texttt{colormatexec}$[\mathit{ncol}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ncol}} read-write
registers containing an array of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits with exec
permissions, used to check branches from executable pages with a
matching register \emph{page table color} to branch addresses in
executable pages with a matching \emph{page table color} index.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  8/{0}//{15}/\emph{exec perms}%
}
\fi

\newpage
\section{Domain extension}

the architecture provides an optional domain~\cite{barham2003xen}
extension for virtual machines.
the domain extension is a component of the privilege and virtual machine
isolation system.
the extension adds registers for virtual domain addresses, trap routing,
page translation, and context switching for system traps, domain traps,
capability traps and machine traps.
\para
the architecture is designed to work without the domain extension.
if the domain extension is not present, the user and system roles are
derived from their respective address space prefixes, and traps are
handled without capability trap routing and domain context switching.
the capability and domain extensions are detected and enabled via the machine extension.
\para
the following table lists the privileged domain system parameters:

\footnotesize

\begin{tabularx}{95mm}{|l|X|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{description} & \thead{value} \\
\hline
$\mathit{ndom}$      & the number of architectural domains & 16 \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{domain parameters}
\label{tab:domain-parameters}
\end{table}

\subsection{domain routing registers}

this section describes the \emph{domain control registers}, which provide
access to the domain stack pointer, the domain number vector, and control
trap routing for context switching of system, domain, capability, machine,
and message traps.

\subsubsection{domain stack pointer (\texttt{domsp})}
\texttt{domsp} is a read-write register containing the domain stack
pointer, which points at the top element in the domain number vector.
\\
\registerspec{32}{32}{%
  3/{0}//{2}/\emph{index}%
}

\subsubsection{domain number vector (\texttt{domvec})}
\texttt{domvec} is a read-write register containing the domain number
stack. the top element in the domain number vector is indexed by
\texttt{domsp} and contains the current domain number.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  4/{0}//{3}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[0]},%
  4////,%
  4/{8}//{11}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[1]},%
  4////,%
  4/{16}//{19}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[2]},%
  4////,%
  4/{24}//{27}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[3]},%
  4////,%
  4/{32}//{35}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[4]},%
  4////,%
  4/{40}//{43}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[5]},%
  4////,%
  4/{48}//{51}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[6]},%
  4////,%
  4/{56}//{59}/\footnotesize\emph{dn[7]}%
}

\subsubsection{domain default routes (\texttt{domroute}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domroute}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing the domain number of the default domain that system,
domain, capability, machine, and message traps are routed to.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  4/{0}//{3}/\emph{sys},%
  4////,%
  4/{8}//{11}/\emph{dom},%
  4////,%
  4/{16}//{19}/\emph{cap},%
  4////,%
  4/{24}//{27}/\emph{mac},%
  4////,%
  4/{32}//{35}/\emph{msg}%
}
