\subsection{domain context registers}

this section describes the \emph{domain context registers} that provide
per-domain storage for trap context, domain addresses, page table
root pointer, and domain trap handlers.

\subsubsection{domain scratch (\texttt{domscratch}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domscratch}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the trap scratch values associated with
each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{GLEN-1}/\emph{trap scratch}%
}

\subsubsection{domain cause (\texttt{domcause}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domcause}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-only
registers containing an array of the trap causes associated with each
domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{GLEN-1}/\emph{trap cause}%
}

\subsubsection{domain value (\texttt{domvalue}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domvalue}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-only
registers containing an array of the trap values associated with each
domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{GLEN-1}/\emph{trap value}%
}

\subsubsection{domain address (\texttt{domaddr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domaddr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing the domain addresses associated with each domain.
domain addresses must be unique within one processor;
otherwise, behavior is implementation-defined.
the domain address prefix should be unary \texttt{1110}.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{domain address}%
}

\subsubsection{domain timer (\texttt{domtimer}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domtimer}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of one-shot time stamp deadlines associated
with each domain. all non-zero timers are active and will deliver to the
message role of the associated domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//63/\emph{time stamp deadline}%
}

\newpage
\subsubsection{domain page table root (\texttt{domptr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domptr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the page table root pointers for
address translation in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{63}/\emph{page table root}%
}

\subsubsection{domain exception program counter (\texttt{domepc}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domepc}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the addresses for the domain trap exception
addresses in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{program counter}%
}

\subsubsection{domain exception immediate base (\texttt{domeib}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domeib}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the addresses for the domain trap exception
constants in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{immediate base}%
}

\subsubsection{domain handler program counter (\texttt{domhpc}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domhpc}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the addresses for the domain trap handler
routines in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{program counter}%
}

\subsubsection{domain handler immediate base (\texttt{domhib}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domhib}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the addresses for the domain trap handler
constants in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{immediate base}%
}

\newpage
\section{Machine extension}

the architecture provides an optional machine extension that includes
registers for control of the capability and domain extensions,
physical memory protection, machine context vectors, software-defined
memory management, and memory type information.
\para
the architecture is designed to work without the machine extension.
if the machine extension is not present, then the system page table
roots use pre-defined formats. when the machine extension is present,
a global TLB miss handler allows custom page table walkers. when the
domain and machine extensions are present, domains specify a machine
default domain in \texttt{domroute[domain].mac}, the machine context for
nested address translation. the machine context is used to route TLB
miss exceptions to per-domain TLB miss handlers.

\subsubsection{machine registers}
the following table lists the privileged machine registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} & \thead{depends} \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\tsub{machine control registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x61} & mcontrol  & machine control & \\
\texttt{0x62} & mfeature  & machine feature & \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\tsub{machine context registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x2000...0x200F} & macaddr$[n]$   & machine address & domain \\
\texttt{0x2100...0x210F} & machpc$[n]$    & machine handler program counter & domain \\
\texttt{0x2200...0x220F} & machib$[n]$    & machine handler immediate base & domain \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\tsub{machine memory registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x63} & mtlbkey   & machine tlb key & \\
\texttt{0x64} & mtlbscope & machine tlb format & \\
\texttt{0x65} & mtlbent   & machine tlb entry & \\
\texttt{0x66} & mtcount   & memory type count & \\
\texttt{0x2300...0x230F}  & mtaddr$[n]$ & memory type address & \\
\texttt{0x2400...0x240F}  & mtmask$[n]$ & memory type mask & \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{machine registers}
\label{tab:machine-registers}
\end{table}

\subsection{machine context registers}
the section describes the privileged machine registers that provide the
per-domain machine address and machine trap handlers for software-defined
memory management.

\subsubsection{machine address (\texttt{macaddr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{macaddr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing the machine addresses associated with each domain.
machine addresses must be unique across all processors; otherwise,
behavior is implementation-defined.
the machine address prefix should be unary \texttt{0001}.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{MLEN-1}/\emph{machine address}%
}

\subsubsection{machine handler program counter (\texttt{machpc}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{machpc}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the domain physical addresses for the
machine trap handler routines in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{program counter}%
}

\subsubsection{machine handler immediate base (\texttt{machib}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{machib}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the domain physical addresses for the
machine trap handler constants in each domain.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  64/{0}//{ALEN-1}/\emph{immediate base}%
}

\newpage
\subsection{machine memory registers}

the section describes the privileged machine registers for software-defined
address translation and memory type information. the address translation
registers enable a \emph{tlb-miss-fault} trap handler that walks page table
trie structure to populate the TLB\footnote{Translation Lookaside Buffer}.
the memory type registers enable configuration of caching and write
propagation behavior for memory ranges.

\subsubsection{machine translation lookaside buffer key (\texttt{mtlbkey})}
\texttt{mtlbkey} is a read-only register containing the translation address
for the current page translation lookaside buffer miss fault to be read by
the TLB miss fault trap handler.
\para
the \emph{vm12d}, \emph{vm14c}, and \emph{vm16c} translation lookaside buffer
key formats are as follows.

  16/{48}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{sext}%
}

  17/{47}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{sext}%
}

\subsubsection{memory type count (\texttt{mtcount})}
\texttt{mtcount} is a read-only register containing the number of
available memory type registers.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  4/{0}//{3}/\footnotesize\emph{count}%
}

\subsubsection{memory type address (\texttt{mtaddr}$[\mathit{n}]$)}
\texttt{mtaddr}$[\mathit{n}]$ are \textbf{\emph{n}} read-write
registers containing physical address and memory type.
when multiple memory types are defined for the same range,
including page table memory types, if address translation
is active, then the resulting memory type will be
$\max(\mathit{type}_1,\mathit{type}_2)$. this conflict resolution
rule preserves the most device-friendly memory type with the least caching.
a conflict resolution override mask can be used when this is not
the desired behavior.
\\
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  8/{0}//7/\footnotesize\emph{reserved},%
  4/{8}//11/\footnotesize\emph{type},%
  40/{12}//51/\footnotesize\emph{physical address},%
  12/{52}//63/\footnotesize\emph{reserved}%
}

\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}

\rowcolor{thcol}

\hline
\texttt{0b0000} & \emph{WB}  & write-back \\
\texttt{0b0001} & \emph{WC}  & write-combine \\
\texttt{0b0010} & \emph{WT}  & write-through \\
\texttt{0b0011} & \emph{UC}  & uncached \\
\texttt{0b0100} & --  & \emph{override mask} \\
\texttt{0b1000} & --  & \emph{reserved mask} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{memory type address memory type field.}
\label{tab:memory-type-address-memory-type-field}
\end{table}

}

\newpage
\section{Array extension}

the array extension defines a 128-bit \emph{array unit descriptor}
constant format containing array unit configuration along with array
load and store instructions for tiled array fetches.

\subsection{tiled array fetch}
the array extension defines tiled array fetch instructions implementing
image load-store, which map to Vulkan and OpenCL images%
~\cite{khronos-vulkan,khronos-opencl}.
Vulkan and OpenCL separate image and sampler objects into distinct types,
where the image object encapsulates the memory layout, dimensions, and
format, and the sampler object contains the filtering, interpolation,
and addressing configuration.
SPIR-V~\cite{khronos-spirv} represents the combined image and sampler
as \texttt{OpTypeSampledImage} and uses \texttt{OpSampleImage} operations
to fetch and filter samples.
\para
in the proposed architecture, filtering and interpolation are lowered
to vector instructions and the tiled array fetch is implemented using
array unit descriptors and instructions.
the array unit descriptor is a 128-bit constant encoding the tiling
geometry, dimension ordering, mipmap levels, component format, and
swizzle for a tiled array resource.
the array unit descriptor can represent Vulkan images in either linear or
optimal tiling, corresponding to images before or after layout transition.
\para
the array unit descriptor is loaded from constant or data memory into a
general register and used to configure the array unit before use.
this enables temporal parallelism for address calculation.
array unit instructions take a base address, a descriptor register, and
a coordinate vector, and compute the final memory address from the tile
geometry and mipmap level encoded in the descriptor. this makes the tiling
layout visible to the compiler, in contrast to Vulkan image layouts,
where tiling is implementation-defined and opaque to the application.

\newpage
\subsection{array unit descriptor}

the array unit descriptor contains the following fields:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{field} & \thead{value} & \thead{bits} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{$\mathsf{order}$}            & row, column, ...  & $3$ & order of array dimensions \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{format}$}           & 8, 16, 32, 64, 128 & $4$ & word size and component format \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{levels}$}           & 0--15         & $4$ & number of \emph{mipmap} levels \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{base}$}             & 0--15         & $4$ & base \emph{mipmap} level of detail \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{swiz}_{[0,1,2,3]}$} & r, g, b, a, 0, 1, 2, -1 & $3 \times 4$ & component swizzle \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{tile}_{[x,y,z]}$}   & 1, 2, 4, 8, ..., 128 & $3 \times 3$ & \emph{power-of-two} size of tiles \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{size}_{[x,y,z]}$}   & 0--8192       & $13 \times 3$ & total number of tiles \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{count}_{[x,y,z]}$}  & 0--8192       & $13 \times 3$ & visible number of tiles \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{offset}_{[x,y]}$}   & 0--127        & $7 \times 2$ & tile coordinate offset \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{array unit descriptor fields}
\label{tab:array-unit-descriptor-fields}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the array unit descriptor limits are as follows:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize

\begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|r|r|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}

\hline
\texttt{$\mathsf{x}$} &   1 &   1 &    8192 & 13 \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{x}$} &  16 &  16 &  131072 & 17 \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{x}$} & 128 & 128 & 1048576 & 20 \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{y}$} &   1 &   1 &    8192 & 13 \\
\texttt{$\mathsf{y}$} &  16 &  16 &  131072 & 17 \\

\texttt{$\mathsf{z}$} &   1 &   1 &    8192 & 13 \\

\subsection{array unit fields}

the following section describes the array unit fields.

\begin{samepage}
\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\subsubsection{order}

\texttt{order} describes the order of array dimensions.
the $\{x,y,z\}$ field orders apply to both the outer tile order
and the order within tiles.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{name} & \thead{order} & \thead{mnemonic} \\
\hline
\texttt{0b000} & \texttt{xyz} & Linear $\{x,y,z\}$ & row-major \\
\texttt{0b001} & \texttt{yxz} & Linear $\{y,x,z\}$ & column-major \\
\texttt{0b010} & \texttt{zxy} & Linear $\{z,x,y\}$ & z-major-row \\
\texttt{0b011} & \texttt{zyx} & Linear $\{z,y,x\}$ & z-major-column \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{array unit order field}
\label{tab:array-unit-order-field}
\end{table}
\end{samepage}

\begin{samepage}
\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\subsubsection{format}

\texttt{format} describes the word size and component format of the array data.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{format} & \thead{type} & \thead{word size} & \thead{components} \\
\hline
\texttt{0b0000} & \texttt{RGBA2}  & \texttt{i2x4}  & 8-bit  & \texttt{2:2:2:2} \\
\texttt{0b0001} & \texttt{RGBA4}  & \texttt{i4x4}  & 16-bit & \texttt{4:4:4:4} \\
\texttt{0b0010} & \texttt{RGBA8}  & \texttt{i8x4}  & 32-bit & \texttt{8:8:8:8} \\
\texttt{0b0011} & \texttt{RGBA16} & \texttt{i16x4} & 64-bit & \texttt{16:16:16:16} \\
\texttt{0b0100} & \texttt{RGBA32} & \texttt{i32x4} & 128-bit & \texttt{32:32:32:32} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{array unit format field}
\label{tab:array-unit-format-field}
\end{table}
\end{samepage}

\newpage
\subsection{array unit instructions}

the array extension requires $\mathit{GLEN}=128$. an array unit descriptor is
loaded into a register using a 128-bit constant load instruction, or to access
descriptor arrays, a 128-bit memory load instruction. the array unit must be
configured with the descriptor before use to allow address computation.
array unit instructions take a base address, an array unit descriptor
register, and a coordinate vector. the following table describes
the high-level templates for the array unit instructions.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{operation name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
configure-array-unit \texttt{rd} &
configure array unit with dimensions in descriptor (\texttt{rd}) \\
get-array-count \texttt{rc} \texttt{rd} &
get array visible size coordinate (\texttt{rc}) from descriptor (\texttt{rd}) \\
get-array-size \texttt{rc} \texttt{rd} &
get array total size coordinate (\texttt{rc}) from descriptor (\texttt{rd}) \\
broadcast-array-count \texttt{vc} \texttt{rd} &
broadcast array visible size coordinate (\texttt{vc}) from descriptor (\texttt{rd}) \\
broadcast-array-size \texttt{vc} \texttt{rd} &
broadcast array total size coordinate (\texttt{vc}) from descriptor (\texttt{rd}) \\
scalar-array-load \texttt{re} \texttt{rc} \texttt{rd} \texttt{rb} \texttt{ra} &
load scalar with desc (\texttt{rd}), base (\texttt{rb}), and coordinate (\texttt{rc}) \\
scalar-array-store \texttt{rd} \texttt{rc} \texttt{rb} \texttt{ra} &
store scalar with desc (\texttt{rd}), base (\texttt{rb}), and coordinate (\texttt{rc}) \\
vector-array-load \texttt{ve} \texttt{rd} \texttt{vc} \texttt{rb} \texttt{va} &
load vector with desc (\texttt{rd}), base (\texttt{rb}), and vector of coordinates (\texttt{vc}) \\
vector-array-store \texttt{rd} \texttt{vc} \texttt{rb} \texttt{va} &
store vector with desc (\texttt{rd}), base (\texttt{rb}), and vector of coordinates (\texttt{vc}) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{array unit instructions}
\label{tab:array-unit-instructions}
\end{table}

\vspace{-1em}

\subsubsection{coordinate vectors}

the 64-bit and 128-bit integer coordinate vector layouts are as follows.

\registerspecrow{32}{32}{0.0}{

  16/{16}//{31}/$y_{[15:0]}$%
}
\registerspecrow{32}{32}{2.0}{
  16/{32}//{47}/$z_{[15:0]}$,%
  4/{48}//{51}/$\mathit{level}_{[3:0]}$,%
  12/{52}//{63}/$\mathit{reserved}$%
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{64-bit integer coordinate vector.}
\label{fig:64-bit-integer-coordinate-vector}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.45,yscale=0.40]
\registerspecrow{32}{32}{0.0}{
  21/{0}//{20}/$x_{[20:0]}$,%
  11/{21}//{31}/$\mathit{reserved}$%
}
\registerspecrow{32}{32}{2.0}{
  21/{32}//{52}/$y_{[20:0]}$,%
  11/{53}//{63}/$\mathit{reserved}$%
}
\registerspecrow{32}{32}{4.0}{
  21/{64}//{84}/$z_{[20:0]}$,%
  11/{85}//{95}/$\mathit{reserved}$%
}
\registerspecrow{32}{32}{6.0}{

\newpage
\subsubsection{component swizzle}

the component swizzle using the array unit descriptor is as follows.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{compute swizzle using array unit descriptor and word}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{compute\_swizzle}{$d,w$}
\Comment{$d$: descriptor, $w$: word}
  \State $\mathit{formats} \gets \{\;1,\;2,\;4,\;8,\;16\;\}$
  \Comment{define unit sizes}
  \State $\mathit{values} \gets \{\;0,\;1,\;2,\;-1\;\}$
  \Comment{define substitutions}
  \State $u \gets \mathit{formats}[d.\mathit{format}] << 1$
  \Comment{get component unit size}
  \State $m \gets (1 << u) - 1$
  \Comment{compute component mask}
  \State $r \gets 0$
  \For{$i \gets 0$ to $3$}
    \Comment{for each component}
    \State $c \gets d.\mathit{swiz}_i$
    \Comment{get component swizzle}
    \State $v \gets (w >> (c \times u)) \;\mathrm{if}\; c < 4\;
       \mathrm{else}\; \mathit{values}[c \land 3]$
    \Comment{get component or substitution}
    \State $r \gets r \lor ((v \land m) << (i \times u))$
    \Comment{accumulate result}
  \EndFor
  \State \Return $r$
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsubsection{mipmap base address}
the mipmap base address calculation using the array unit descriptor is as follows.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{compute mipmap base address using array unit descriptor and level}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{compute\_mipmap\_base}{$d,l,k=0$}
\Comment{$d$: descriptor, $l$: mipmap level, $k$: current depth}

  \If{$k = l \lor k \ge \mathit{d.levels}$}
    \State \Return $0$
  \EndIf

  \State $s \gets \mathit{d.tile}$
  \Comment{get tile shift}
  \State $p \gets (\mathit{d.size} >> k) + 1$
  \Comment{get tile pitch}

  \State $q \gets
    \left\{ 1 << s_x, \;
            1 << s_y, \;
            1 << s_z \right\}$
  \Comment{compute tile size}
  \State $i_x \gets u,\;
          i_y \gets q_x \times i_x,\;
          i_z \gets q_y \times i_y$
  \Comment{compute tile stride}
  \State $j_x \gets i_z,\;
          j_y \gets p_x \times j_x,\;
          j_z \gets p_y \times j_y$
  \Comment{compute array stride}

  \State \Return $j_z + \Call{compute\_mipmap\_base}{d,l,k+1}$
  \Comment{return base address}

\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\subsubsection{array dimensions}

the array dimensions are computed from the array unit descriptor as follows:
\\
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{dim} &
\thead{array total size} (\texttt{size}) &
\thead{array visible size} (\texttt{count}) \\
\hline
x & $(\mathit{d.size}_x + 1) \times (1 << \mathit{d.tile}_x)$ &
    $(\mathit{d.count}_x + 1) \times (1 << \mathit{d.tile}_x) - \mathit{d.offset}_x$ \\
y & $(\mathit{d.size}_y + 1) \times (1 << \mathit{d.tile}_y)$ &
    $(\mathit{d.count}_y + 1) \times (1 << \mathit{d.tile}_y) - \mathit{d.offset}_y$ \\
z & $(\mathit{d.size}_z + 1) \times (1 << \mathit{d.tile}_z)$ &
    $(\mathit{d.count}_z + 1) \times (1 << \mathit{d.tile}_z)$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the coordinate field order is swapped using the array unit descriptor as follows:

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{swap field order using array unit descriptor and coordinate vector}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{swap\_fields}{$d,v$}
\Comment{$d$: descriptor, $v$: coordinates}

  \State \textbf{case} $\mathit{d.order}$ \textbf{of}
  \State \hspace{1em} $\mathsf{xyz}$: $v \gets \{ v_x, v_y, v_z, v_w \}$
  \State \hspace{1em} $\mathsf{yxz}$: $v \gets \{ v_y, v_x, v_z, v_w \}$
  \State \hspace{1em} $\mathsf{zxy}$: $v \gets \{ v_z, v_x, v_y, v_w \}$
  \State \hspace{1em} $\mathsf{zyx}$: $v \gets \{ v_z, v_y, v_x, v_w \}$
  \State \textbf{end case}

  \State \Return $v$

\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsubsection{address calculation}

the array access address calculation using the array unit descriptor is as follows.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{compute address using array unit descriptor and coordinate vector}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{compute\_address}{$d,b,v$}
\Comment{$d$: descriptor, $b$: base address, $v$: coordinates}

  \State $\mathit{formats} \gets \{\;1,\;2,\;4,\;8,\;16\;\}$
  \Comment{define unit sizes}
  \State $u \gets \mathit{formats}[\mathit{d.format}]$
  \Comment{get unit size}

  \State $l \gets \mathit{desc.base} + (v_{level} \land 15)$
  \Comment{get mipmap level}
  \State $s \gets \Call{swap\_fields}{d, \mathit{d.tile}}$
  \Comment{get tile shift swapped}
  \State $p \gets \Call{swap\_fields}{d, (\mathit{d.size} >> l) + 1}$
  \Comment{get tile pitch swapped}
  \State $v \gets \Call{swap\_fields}{d,v}$
  \Comment{get coordinates swapped}

  \State $r \gets b + \Call{compute\_mipmap\_base}{d,l}$
  \Comment{compute mipmap base}

  \State $q \gets \left\{ 1 << s_x, \; 1 << s_y, \; 1 << s_z \right\}$
  \Comment{compute tile size}
  \State $m \gets \left\{  q_x - 1, \;  q_y - 1, \;  q_z - 1 \right\}$
  \Comment{compute tile mask}

  \State $i_x \gets u,\;
          i_y \gets q_x \times i_x,\;
          i_z \gets q_y \times i_y$
  \Comment{compute tile stride}
  \State $j_x \gets i_z,\;
          j_y \gets p_x \times j_x,\;
          j_z \gets p_y \times j_y$
  \Comment{compute array stride}

  \State $f \gets \left\{ v_x >> l, \; v_y >> l, \; v_z >> l \right\}$
  \Comment{compute final offset}
  \State $t \gets
    \left\{ f_x \land m_x, \;
            f_y \land m_y, \;
            f_z \land m_z \right\}$
  \Comment{compute tile offset}
  \State $a \gets
    \left\{ f_x >> s_x, \;
            f_y >> s_y, \;
            f_z >> s_z \right\}$
  \Comment{compute array offset}

  \State \Return $r +
    t_x \times i_x +
    t_y \times i_y +
    t_z \times i_z +
    a_x \times j_x +
    a_y \times j_y +
    a_z \times j_z$
  \Comment{return final address}

\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

%
% array extension end
%
\fi

\addtocontents{toc}{\protect\clearpage}
\chapter{System operation}

this chapter describes the operational semantics of the architecture.
this includes architectural behavior in response to privileged instructions,
interrupts, memory management faults, capability violations, and updates to
system and machine state.
this chapter also describes interaction between the processor core,
interrupt routing fabric, virtual domains, memory management system,
and virtual machine environment.
\para
the system operational semantics are divided into the following sections.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|X|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\textbf{interrupt handling} & synchronous and asynchronous traps and
exceptions, trap routing, and domain context switching for system,
domain, capability, machine, and message traps. \\
\textbf{memory management} & page table structure, page table formats,
page table walking, address translation, and memory access permission checks. \\
\textbf{security model} & user, system, capability, domain, and machine
roles, permissions, address space isolation, and capability exceptions. \\
\textbf{virtual machine} & hypervisor, firmware interfaces, hardware
description models, and virtual devices for I/O. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{system operation}
\label{tab:system-operation}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Interrupt handling}

the interrupt system is designed to support synchronous and
asynchronous traps to divert control flow for instruction execution
exceptions and synchronous or asynchronous message delivery interrupts
for inter-domain messages, timers, and peripheral interrupt pins.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item synchronous traps due to instruction execution.
\item synchronous traps due to locally-scoped inter-domain messages.
\item asynchronous traps due to interrupts from timers or interrupt pins.
\item asynchronous traps due to globally-scoped inter-domain messages.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{trap handlers}

a trap handler is the control flow entry point to a function that handles
synchronous traps caused by instruction execution faults and asynchronous
traps caused by external interrupts. when a trap is received the processor
saves the \emph{program counter} and \emph{immediate base} registers into
\{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \}, then branches to the addresses in
\{ \texttt{thpc}, \texttt{thib} \}.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{register}      & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib}     & address of the trapping instruction. \\
\texttt{thpc}, \texttt{thib}     & address of the trap handler. \\
\texttt{tstatus}                 & asynchronous interrupts pending. \\
\texttt{tcontrol} & asynchronous interrupts enable. \\
\texttt{tscratch} & scratch register for the trap handler. \\
\texttt{tcause}   & cause of the current trap. \\
\texttt{tvalue}   & identity of the current trap. \\
\texttt{tack}     & acknowledgement of the current trap. \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap handler registers}
\label{tab:trap-handler-registers}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{synchronous traps}

synchronous traps arise from instruction execution events such as:
instruction execution permissions, illegal instruction exceptions,
breakpoint exceptions, debug monitor exceptions, address alignment
faults, memory access faults, and page faults.
when synchronous traps occur, \{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \} contain
the address of the instruction that caused the trap.

\subsubsection{asynchronous traps}

asynchronous traps are generated by external events such as: interrupt
pins, message-signaled interrupts from other threads or domains on the
system, or deadline timer interrupts. when asynchronous traps occur,
\{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \} contain the address of the
instruction that was interrupted and must be returned to after
processing of the asynchronous trap.

\subsection{trap routing}

capability and domain registers are used to configure the trap routing
domains for context switches arising from interrupts and exceptions.
trap routing is configured by the hypervisor in the \texttt{domroute}
vector, which contain the domain ids of domains that receive traps
for the \emph{system}, \emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine},
and \emph{message} roles respectively.
synchronous and asynchronous interrupts and exceptions are delivered
to the \emph{system}, \emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine},
and \emph{message} roles depending on the initiating event:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{role} & \thead{exception category} \\
\hline
\emph{system}     & system exceptions. \\
\emph{domain}     & domain exceptions. \\
\emph{capability} & capability exceptions. \\
\emph{machine}    & machine exceptions. \\
\emph{message}    & message exceptions. \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap routing roles}
\label{tab:trap-routing-roles}
\end{table}

\vspace{-1em}

\subsection{synchronous exceptions}

synchronous exceptions use the \emph{system}, \emph{domain},
\emph{capability}, and \emph{machine} roles and are routed based on
domain numbers in the \texttt{sys}, \texttt{dom}, \texttt{cap}, and
\texttt{mac} fields of the \texttt{domroute} vector:
\para
\textbf{system exceptions:}
arising from access to system registers, illegal instruction
exceptions, breakpoint exceptions, debug monitor exceptions,
address alignment faults, memory access faults, page faults arising
from page table entry configuration, and locally-scoped
\emph{system role} messages, also known as system calls.
\para
\textbf{capability exceptions:}
arising from access to capability registers, access checks due
to the configuration of the capability permission registers,
\iffalse
capability role registers, and the capability matrix,
\fi
and locally-scoped \emph{capability role} messages, also known as
capability calls.
\para
\textbf{domain exceptions:}
arising from access to domain registers, including the trap routing
registers, and locally-scoped \emph{domain role} messages, also known
as hyper calls.
\para
\textbf{machine exceptions:}
faults arising from access to machine registers, translation lookaside
buffer refill faults, and locally-scoped \emph{machine role} messages,
also known as machine calls.

\subsection{asynchronous exceptions}

asynchronous exceptions use the \emph{message} role and are
routed based on domain number in the \texttt{msg} field of the
\texttt{domroute} vector:
\para
\textbf{message exceptions:}
arising from interrupt pins, the deadline timer, and
globally-scoped inter-domain messages, also known as message-signaled
interrupts.

\newpage
\subsection{inter-domain messages}

inter-domain messages are used to send messages to threads locally
or globally. inter-domain messages are grouped into two categories.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item locally-scoped synchronous inter-domain messages.
\item globally-scoped asynchronous inter-domain messages.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{locally-scoped inter-domain messages}
\emph{locally-scoped inter-domain messages} route synchronous traps
generated by message instructions to domains on the local processor.
they are used to invoke functions in isolated domain contexts under
different roles. the trap routing table \texttt{domroute} indicates
the domain to handle the trap based on role.
the following table shows the inter-domain message instructions:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{role}      & \thead{call}    & \thead{return} \\
\hline
\emph{system}     & \texttt{syscall} & \texttt{iret} \\
\emph{domain}     & \texttt{domcall} & \texttt{iret} \\
\emph{capability} & \texttt{capcall} & \texttt{iret} \\
\emph{machine}    & \texttt{maccall} & \texttt{iret} \\
\emph{message}    & \texttt{msgcall} & \texttt{iret} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{inter-domain message instructions}
\label{tab:inter-domain-message-instructions}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{globally-scoped inter-domain messages}
\emph{globally-scoped inter-domain messages} route asynchronous
message exceptions to the message default domain on remote processors.
the \texttt{starget}, \texttt{smask}, and \texttt{smessage}
system registers are used to multicast asynchronous message interrupts
over the processor network. interrupts are delivered to processors
matching the advertised machine or domain addresses after applying
an address mask. \texttt{sstatus.E} signals message interrupt queuing
exceptions due to \emph{head-of-line blocking} or other transport
faults. \emph{machine addresses} are unique to one processor.
\emph{domain addresses} may be advertised by multiple processors.
either address type may use masked comparison to select multiple
destinations for reliable multicast delivery:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \textbf{machine addresses:} physically identify a unique thread
and domain number.
\item \textbf{domain addresses:} logically identify threads sharing the
same domain address.
\end{itemize}

\newpage
\subsection{wired interrupt pins}

there are 32 wired interrupt pins per processor. an interrupt is
generated upon an edge condition on the lowest-numbered interrupt pin
that is asserted. on the signaled processor, asynchronous traps for
wired interrupts are unconditionally routed to domain 0\footnote{wired
interrupts are routed to domain 0 because there is currently no mechanism
to route them elsewhere. message interrupts must be used for virtual
devices in domains besides domain 0.}. the processor initiates a
\emph{domain context switch} to domain 0 and then branches to its trap
handler. \texttt{tcause} is set to \emph{interrupt-n} where \emph{n} is
the pin number, and \texttt{tvalue} is set to zero.

\subsection{deadline timer interrupts}

deadline timer interrupts can be configured by setting the \texttt{stimer}
register. an interrupt is generated when $\mathtt{stimer} \ge \mathtt{stamp}$.
on the signaled processor, \texttt{domroute[domain].msg}
contains the message default domain used to route an asynchronous trap.
the processor initiates a \emph{domain context switch} to the
\emph{message default domain}, then branches to its trap handler.
\texttt{tcause} is set to \emph{timer-interrupt} and \texttt{tvalue} is set
to the value in the \texttt{stamp} register.

\subsection{message signaled interrupts}

message-signaled interrupts, also known as globally-scoped inter-domain
messages, are multicast over the processor network to initiate asynchronous
exceptions in the message role of all threads that advertise the target
thread or domain address after applying a mask.
\para
the \emph{system} role of the initiating processor first disables
interrupts, then sets the \texttt{starget} and \texttt{smask} registers
with a target machine or domain address and mask, followed by writing to
the \texttt{smessage} register. a \emph{message interrupt} is then multicast
to the set of threads matching the thread or domain address applying the mask.
\texttt{sstatus.E} is set if an exception occurs.
\para
the following special addresses can be used:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{address} & \thead{type} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{0\ldots0}& all-zeros & \emph{boot processor} \\
\texttt{1\ldots1} & all-ones & \emph{broadcast address} \\
\texttt{0001?} & link-layer & \emph{machine address} \\
\texttt{1110?} & group-layer & \emph{domain address} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{special inter-domain message addresses}
\label{tab:inter-domain-message-addresses}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
on signaled processors, \texttt{domroute[domain].msg} contains the
message default domain used to route an asynchronous trap.
the processor initiates a \emph{domain context switch} to the
\emph{message default domain}, then branches to its trap handler.
\texttt{tcause} is set to \emph{message-interrupt} and \texttt{tvalue} is
set to the masked thread or domain address from the initiating processor.

\newpage
\subsection{domain context switching}

interrupt delivery due to synchronous and asynchronous exceptions causes
a context switch to the trap routing default domain for the role the
exception is routed to for the current domain: the \emph{system},
\emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine}, and \emph{message}
roles. the following table shows inter-domain trap routing table entries:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{role}      & \thead{trap routing default domain} \\
\hline
\emph{system}     & \texttt{domroute[domain].sys} \\
\emph{domain}     & \texttt{domroute[domain].dom} \\
\emph{capability} & \texttt{domroute[domain].cap} \\
\emph{machine}    & \texttt{domroute[domain].mac} \\
\emph{message}    & \texttt{domroute[domain].msg} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{inter-domain trap routing table entries}
\label{tab:inter-domain-trap-routing-table-entries}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the top element in the domain number vector \texttt{domvec}, indexed by
the domain stack pointer \texttt{domsp}, holds the current domain number.
the remaining elements contain the domain numbers of suspended domains,
forming a stack of nested exception contexts.
if the default domain number for a role is configured as self-mapped,
then its traps will be delivered in the current domain. the privileged
registers are aliases of the domain context registers for the current domain.
the following register arrays hold state for a domain context switch:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{domain register} & \thead{privileged register} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{domscratch[n]} & \texttt{tscratch} & trap scratch \\
\texttt{domcause[n]} & \texttt{tcause} & trap cause \\
\texttt{domvalue[n]} & \texttt{tvalue} & trap value \\
\texttt{domepc[n]} & \texttt{tepc} & trap exception program counter \\
\texttt{domeib[n]} & \texttt{teib} & trap exception immediate base \\
\texttt{domhpc[n]} & \texttt{thpc} & trap handler program counter \\
\texttt{domhib[n]} & \texttt{thib} & trap handler immediate base \\
\texttt{macaddr[n]} & \texttt{saddress} & system machine address \\
\texttt{domaddr[n]} & \texttt{sdomain} & system domain address \\
\texttt{domtimer[n]} & \texttt{stimer} & system deadline timer \\
\texttt{domptr[n]} & \texttt{sptr} & system page table root \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{domain context switch registers}
\label{tab:domain-context-switch-registers}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
when domain context switch is enabled in \texttt{domcontrol.D}, system
traps use the \emph{domain\_trap} and \emph{domain\_return} procedures to
enable domain context stacking when delivering and return from traps.
when domain contexts are disabled in \texttt{domcontrol.D}, or if the
domain extension is not present, system traps use the \emph{system\_trap}
and \emph{system\_return} procedures to deliver and return from traps.

\newpage
\subsubsection{system trap}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is performed atomically to issue a system trap.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{execute system trap handler}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{system\_trap}{$\mathit{cause,value}$}
    \State $\mathit{tcontrol.I} \gets \mathit{FALSE}$
    \Comment{disable system interrupts}
    \State $\mathit{tcause} \gets \mathit{cause}$
    \Comment{save trap cause}
    \State $\mathit{tvalue} \gets \mathit{value}$
    \Comment{save trap value}
    \State $\mathit{tepc} \gets \mathit{pc}$
    \Comment{save exception program counter}
    \State $\mathit{teib} \gets \mathit{ib}$
    \Comment{save exception immediate base}
    \State $\mathit{pc} \gets \mathit{thpc}$
    \Comment{set program counter to trap handler}
    \State $\mathit{ib} \gets \mathit{thib}$
    \Comment{set immediate base to trap handler}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsubsection{system return}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is performed atomically to issue a system return.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{return to the point of system exception}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{system\_return}{$\mathit{}$}
    \State $\mathit{pc} \gets \mathit{tepc}$
    \Comment{restore program counter register}
    \State $\mathit{ib} \gets \mathit{teib}$
    \Comment{restore immediate base register}
    \State $\mathit{tcontrol.I} \gets \mathit{TRUE}$
    \Comment{enable system interrupts}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsubsection{domain switch}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is performed atomically to cause a domain switch.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{switch system state to new domain}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{domain\_switch}{$\mathit{domain}$}
    \State $\mathit{tscratch}\gets \mathit{domscratch[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap scratch}
    \State $\mathit{tcause}\gets \mathit{domcause[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap cause}
    \State $\mathit{tvalue} \gets \mathit{domvalue[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap value}
    \State $\mathit{tepc} \gets \mathit{domepc[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap exception program counter}
    \State $\mathit{teib} \gets \mathit{domeib[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap exception immediate base}
    \State $\mathit{thpc} \gets \mathit{domhpc[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap handler program counter}
    \State $\mathit{thib} \gets \mathit{domhib[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch trap handler immediate base}
    \State $\mathit{saddress} \gets \mathit{macaddr[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch system machine address}
    \State $\mathit{sdomain} \gets \mathit{domaddr[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch system domain address}
    \State $\mathit{stimer} \gets \mathit{domtimer[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch system deadline timer}
    \State $\mathit{sptr} \gets \mathit{domptr[domain]}$
    \Comment{switch system page table root}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\subsubsection{domain route}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is used to lookup the domain number for a capability role.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{return domain number for a capability role}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{domain\_route}{$\mathit{domain},\mathit{role}$}
    \State \Return \textbf{case} $\mathit{role}$ \textbf{of}
    \State \hspace{1em} $\mathtt{sys}$: $\mathit{domroute[domain].sys}$
    \State \hspace{1em} $\mathtt{dom}$: $\mathit{domroute[domain].dom}$
    \State \hspace{1em} $\mathtt{cap}$: $\mathit{domroute[domain].cap}$
    \State \hspace{1em} $\mathtt{mac}$: $\mathit{domroute[domain].mac}$
    \State \hspace{1em} $\mathtt{msg}$: $\mathit{domroute[domain].msg}$
    \State \textbf{end case}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsubsection{domain trap}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is performed atomically to issue a domain trap.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{context switch to a domain and execute its trap handler}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{domain\_trap}{$\mathit{role,cause,value}$}
    \If {$\mathit{domsp} = \mathit{maxdom} - 1$}
    \Comment{check domain stack pointer}
        \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{domain-fault}
        \Comment{raise domain fault}
    \EndIf
    \State $\mathit{domain} \gets \Call{domain\_route}{\mathit{domvec[domsp]},\mathit{role}}$
    \Comment{get role default domain number}
    \State $\mathit{domvec}[{+\!+}\mathit{domsp}] \gets \mathit{domain}$
    \Comment{push domain number onto domain stack}
    \State $\Call{domain\_switch}{\mathit{domain}}$
    \Comment{perform domain switch}
    \State $\Call{system\_trap}{\mathit{cause},\mathit{value}}$
    \Comment{perform system trap}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsubsection{domain return}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is performed atomically to issue a domain return.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{return to the calling domain at the point of exception}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{domain\_return}{$\mathit{}$}
    \If {$\mathit{domsp} = 0$}
    \Comment{check domain stack pointer}
        \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{domain-fault}
        \Comment{raise domain fault}
    \EndIf
    \State $\mathit{domvec[domsp]} \gets 0$
    \Comment{clear last domain number}
    \State $\mathit{domain} \gets \mathit{domvec}[{-\!-}\mathit{domsp}]$
    \Comment{pop domain number from domain stack}
    \State $\Call{system\_return}{\mathit{}}$
    \Comment{perform system return}
    \State $\Call{domain\_switch}{\mathit{domain}}$
    \Comment{perform domain switch}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\subsection{domain message router}

asynchronous interrupt messages are routed across the processor network
using route tables that aggregate address advertisements.
globally-scoped inter-domain messages carry an address and mask.
the address may be either a \emph{machine address} or a \emph{domain address}.
the use of a mask allows targeting multiple addresses with a single message.
\para
machine addresses are unique across all processors.
domain addresses may alias across processors.
both address types support multicast.
multicast to multiple machine addresses uses masked comparison.
domain addresses are intrinsically multicast and may additionally use
masked comparison to select multiple domains. at most one domain is
matched per processor. if multiple addresses match on one processor,
the lowest-numbered address is selected.
\para
the processor network must provide reliable multicast.
output queues may experience \emph{head-of-line blocking}, which
must be signaled to senders. receivers acknowledge each interrupt.
the router associates a destination count with each queued message
in the sender's output queue, initialized to the number of matching
destinations and decremented on each acknowledgement; the message is
dequeued when the count reaches zero.
multicast delivery should be synchronized based on the maximum
hop count, but is implementation-defined.
\para
the following diagram shows routing tables for an example four node
processor network.
\\

\newpage
\section{Memory management}

the architecture provides for page-based virtual memory%
~\cite{denning1970virtual}. the virtual memory system provides virtual to
physical address translation using a \emph{page table trie} composed of
index pages containing arrays of page table entries. a page walker reads
the structure from the root pointer to leaf entries to translate virtual
addresses into physical addresses. the architecture introduces the
concept of a \emph{translation address} which is an address boxed with
an \emph{address space prefix} (\texttt{AS}) designed to provide a
canonical address form for user and system virtual addresses as
well as physical addresses.
\para
the address translation system is designed to support the following use cases:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item logical partitioning of memory using page table colors and physical self-maps.
\item zoning of page table pages using page table entry translate permissions.
\item tagged translation lookaside buffers using address space identifiers.
\item multiple pages sizes including 4~KiB, 16~KiB, and 64~KiB leaf page sizes.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the follow table describes the dimensions for memory address translation
using the \emph{trie-based} page table structure pointed to by the
\emph{system page table root} (\texttt{sptr}) register.
the page table formats \emph{vm12d}, \emph{vm14c}, and \emph{vm16c} support
4~KiB, 16~KiB, and 64~KiB leaf pages, respectively.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Page} & \thead{Page} & \thead{Page} & \thead{Page} & \thead{Virtual} &
\thead{Page} & & \\
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Table} & \thead{Table} & \thead{No.} & \thead{Offset} & \thead{Address} &
\thead{Index} & \thead{Page} & \thead{Address}  \\
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Format} & \thead{Levels} & \thead{Bits} & \thead{Bits} & \thead{Bits} &
\thead{Entries} & \thead{Sizes} & \thead{Space} \\
\hline
vm12d & 4 & 9 & 12 & 48 & 512 & 4~KiB, 2~MiB, 1~GiB & 256~TiB \\
vm14c & 3 & 11 & 14 & 47 & 2048 & 16~KiB, 32~MiB & 128~TiB \\
vm16c & 3 & 13 & 16 & 55 & 8192 & 64~KiB, 512~MiB & 32~PiB \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{64-bit page table dimensions}
\label{tab:64-bit-page-table-dimensions}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{page table root}

the page table root pointer is stored in the \texttt{sptr} register.
the page walker reads the root pointer to lookup virtual addresses
in the page table trie structure. the page table root pointer contains
a \texttt{fmt} field indicating the page table format, the page table
root physical page number, and an address space identifier used to index
the translation lookaside buffer.
\para
the \emph{vm12d}, \emph{vm14c}, and \emph{vm16c} page table root formats
are as follows.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  12/{0}//{11}/\footnotesize\emph{ASID},%
  40/{12}//{51}/\footnotesize\emph{physical page number},%
  8/{52}//{59}/\footnotesize\emph{reserved},%
  4/{60}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{fmt}%
}
\caption{64-bit \emph{vm12d} page table root format.}
\label{fig:64-bit-vm12d-page-table-root-format}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  12/{0}//{11}/\footnotesize\emph{ASID},%
  2//{13}//\footnotesize\texttt{00},%
  38/{14}//{51}/\footnotesize\emph{physical page number},%
  8/{52}//{59}/\footnotesize\emph{reserved},%
  4/{60}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{fmt}%
}
\caption{64-bit \emph{vm14c} page table root format.}
\label{fig:64-bit-vm14c-page-table-root-format}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  12/{0}//{11}/\footnotesize\emph{ASID},%
  4/{12}//{15}/\footnotesize\texttt{0000},%
  36/{16}//{51}/\footnotesize\emph{physical page number},%
  8/{52}//{59}/\footnotesize\emph{reserved},%
  4/{60}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{fmt}%
}
\caption{64-bit \emph{vm16c} page table root format.}
\label{fig:64-bit-vm16c-page-table-root-format}
\end{figure}

\par\noindent
the supported page table formats are indicated in the \emph{system page table
info} (\texttt{spti}) register.
the page table formats used in the \texttt{fmt} field of the page table root
register are as follows:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{no.} & \thead{type} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{0b0010} & \texttt{vm12d} & 4-level page tables with 4~KiB leaf pages \\
\texttt{0b0100} & \texttt{vm14c} & 3-level page tables with 16~KiB leaf pages \\
\texttt{0b1000} & \texttt{vm16c} & 3-level page tables with 64~KiB leaf pages \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{64-bit page table formats.}
\label{tab:64-bit-page-table-formats}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{page table entries}

page table entries are grouped into arrays within index pages selected by
an index derived from a portion of the virtual address.
each entry contains page color, physical page number, memory type, plus
permission and metadata bits. the physical page number points to the next
page table level for \emph{pointer} entries or the translated physical
address for \emph{leaf} entries.
\para
the \emph{vm12d}, \emph{vm14c}, and \emph{vm16c} page table entry formats
are as follows.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\scriptsize\texttt{V},%
  1//{1}//\scriptsize\texttt{R},%
  1//{2}//\scriptsize\texttt{W},%
  1//{3}//\scriptsize\texttt{X},%
  1//{4}//\scriptsize\texttt{T},%
  1//{5}//\scriptsize\texttt{G},%
  1//{6}//\scriptsize\texttt{A},%
  1//{7}//\scriptsize\texttt{D},%
  4/{8}//{11}/\footnotesize\emph{type},%
  40/{12}//{51}/\footnotesize\emph{physical page number},%
  4/{52}//{55}/\footnotesize\emph{color},%
  8/{56}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{reserved}%
}
\caption{64-bit \emph{vm12d} page table entry format.}
\label{fig:64-bit-vm12d-pte-format}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\registerspec{64}{32}{%
  1//{0}//\scriptsize\texttt{V},%

\subsection{translation addresses}

translation addresses are used by the address translation system to abstract
virtual and physical addresses. translation addresses contain an address
encoded using a page table format specific address layout, where the address
is prefixed with a 1 --- 4 bit sign-extended address space prefix to
distinguish user and system virtual addresses from physical addresses.
\para
the \emph{vm12d}, \emph{vm14c}, and \emph{vm16c} translation address formats
are as follows.
\\

\centering
\registerspec{64}{32}{%

  32/{12}//{43}/\footnotesize\emph{virtual page number},%
  -4/{44}//{47}/\texttt{AS},%
  16/{48}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{sext}%
}
\caption{64-bit \emph{vm12d} translation address format.}
\label{fig:64-bit-vm12d-translation-address-format}
\end{figure}

  29/{14}//{42}/\footnotesize\emph{virtual page number},%
  -4/{43}//{46}/\texttt{AS},%
  17/{47}//{63}/\footnotesize\emph{sext}%
}
\caption{64-bit \emph{vm14c} translation address format.}
\label{fig:64-bit-vm14c-translation-address-format}
\end{figure}

\subsection{translation indices}

translation virtual addresses are decomposed into page table indexing
structures containing a page offset and a vector of page number
indices used to select page table entries in the page table trie.
the page table indexing structure partitions the virtual page
number into a vector of fixed-width page number indices, where
each index has a width of \emph{page number bits}.

structures are as follows.

\newpage
\subsection{page table walker}

the page table walker performs page table lookup to translate virtual
addresses into physical addresses.
the page table walker reads the page table root pointer then walks the
\emph{trie-based} page table structure to find a leaf entry containing
a physical page address.
the translation address must be a virtual address.
the page table root address must be a physical address.
\para
page table entries with the \emph{read, write} and \emph{exec} permission
bits clear are interpreted as \emph{pointer} entries and contain a pointer
to the next-level index page, otherwise they are interpreted as \emph{leaf}
entries with a final translation. entries with the \emph{write} bit set
must have the \emph{read} bit set.
\para
if the physical permissions feature \emph{mfeature.P} is present and
\emph{mcontrol.P} is enabled, then physical pages must be present in the
page table with a self-mapping of their physical address prefixed with
$(\mathit{AS} = \mathit{physical})$ leading to a leaf page table entry
containing its own address. this is to allow physical page permissions
to be checked. in addition to this, the physical self-mappings for physical
pages containing page table pages must have the \emph{translate} bit set.
\para
the page table walker process is as follows.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{find page table entry for translation address}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{page\_walk}{$\mathit{type},\mathit{ppn},\mathit{ta}$}
  \For{$\mathit{level} \gets \mathit{num\_levels} - 1$ to $0$}
    \Comment{for each page table level}
    \State $\mathit{shift} \gets \mathit{level} \times
      \mathit{pn\_bits} + \mathit{po\_bits}$
    \Comment{calculate page number shift}
    \State $\mathit{tpn} \gets (\mathit{ta} >> \mathit{shift}) \land
      ((1 << \mathit{pn\_bits}) - 1)$
    \Comment{calculate translation page number}
    \State $\mathit{pte} \gets \Call{load}{(\mathit{ppn}
      << \mathit{po\_bits}) + \mathit{tpn} \times \mathit{sizeof(PTE)}}$
    \Comment{load page table entry}
    \State $\mathit{leaf} \gets
      \mathit{pte.R} \lor \mathit{pte.W} \lor \mathit{pte.X}$
    \Comment{page table entry node type}
    \State $\mathit{sm} \gets
      \mathit{R} \lor \mathit{W} \lor \mathit{X}$
    \Comment{default self map page table entry}
    \If {$\mathit{mfeature.P} \land \mathit{mcontrol.P} \land \mathit{type} =
        \mathit{virtual}$}
      \State $\mathit{pa} \gets \mathit{pte.ppn} << \mathit{po\_shift}$
      \State $(\mathit{sm},\mathit{level}) \gets \Call{page\_walk}{%
        \mathit{physical},\mathit{sptr.ppn},\mathit{pa}}$
      \Comment{load self mapping}
      \If {$(\mathit{leaf} \land \mathit{pte.ppn} \ne \mathit{sm.ppn}) \lor
        (\lnot\mathit{leaf} \land \lnot\mathit{sm.T})$}
        \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
        \Comment{invalid self mapping}
      \EndIf
    \EndIf
    \If {$\mathit{leaf}$}
      \State \Return $(\mathit{pte}, \mathit{sm}, \mathit{level})$
      \Comment{return page table entry}
    \ElsIf {$\mathit{level} = 0$}
      \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
      \Comment{leaf entry not found}
    \EndIf
  \EndFor
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\subsection{address translation}

address translation, in the context of a memory operation, is the process of
walking the \emph{trie-based} page table structure to locate a page table
entry, then using the physical page number contained within it to translate
a virtual address to a physical address. permission and metadata bits
of the resulting page table entry are checked against the memory operation.
physical self-map permissions, if enabled, are checked. address space
restrictions using the \emph{user address space access disable bit} or
\emph{user address space execute disable bit} are checked.
\para
page table entries without the \emph{accessed} bit set will fault on reads.
page table entries without the \emph{dirty} bit set will fault on writes.
the \emph{global} bit prevents \emph{sptr.ASID} from being associated with
TLB entries.
the \emph{color} field is reserved for use by the capabilities extension.
\para
the virtual to physical address translation process is as follows.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{translate virtual address to physical address}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{virtual\_to\_physical}{$\mathit{op}, \mathit{va}$}
  \Comment{$\mathit{op}$: operation, $\mathit{va}$: virtual address}
  \If {$\mathit{sptr.as} \ne \mathit{physical}$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{page table root is not physical}
  \EndIf
  \State $(\mathit{pte}, \mathit{sm}, \mathit{level}) \gets \Call{page\_walk}{
    \mathit{virtual},\mathit{sptr.ppn},\mathit{va}}$
  \Comment{find page table entry}
  \State $\mathit{shift} \gets \mathit{level} \times
    \mathit{pn\_bits} + \mathit{po\_bits}$
  \Comment{calculate page number shift}
  \State $\mathit{mask} \gets (1 << \mathit{shift}) - 1$
  \State $\mathit{col} \gets
    (\mathit{R} \land \mathit{colorread[pte.color]})
    \lor (\mathit{W} \land \mathit{colorwrite[pte.color]})
    %\lor (\mathit{X} \land \mathit{colorexec[pte.color]})
    $
  \State $\mathit{user} \gets
    \lnot((\mathit{va} >> (\mathit{va\_bits} - 1)) \land 1)$
  \If {$(\lnot\mathit{pte.V}) \lor (\mathit{pte.W} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.R})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid write must have read}
  \ElsIf{$(\mathit{op.R} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.R}) \lor (\mathit{op.W} \land
    \lnot\mathit{pte.W}) \lor (\mathit{op.X} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.X})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid page table permissions}
  \ElsIf{$\mathit{mcontrol.P} \land
     ((\mathit{pte.R} \land \lnot\mathit{sm.R})
     \lor (\mathit{pte.W} \land \lnot\mathit{sm.W})
     \lor (\mathit{pte.X} \land \lnot\mathit{sm.X}))$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid self map permissions}
  \ElsIf{$\mathit{capcontrol.P} \land
    ((\mathit{pte.R} \land \lnot\mathit{col.R})
    \lor (\mathit{pte.W} \land \lnot\mathit{col.W})
    %\lor (\mathit{pte.X} \land \lnot\mathit{col.X})
    )$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid color permissions}
  \ElsIf {$(\mathit{op.R} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.A}) \lor
      (\mathit{op.W} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.D})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid accessed or dirty}
  \ElsIf {$\lnot\mathit{role(user)} \land \lnot\mathit{scontrol.E} \land
      \mathit{user} \land \mathit{op.X}$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid user page execute}
  \ElsIf {$\lnot\mathit{role(user)} \land \lnot\mathit{scontrol.A} \land
      \mathit{user} \land \mathit{op.(R \lor W})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid user page access}
  \ElsIf {($\mathit{pte.ppn} << \mathit{shift}) \land \mathit{mask} \ne 0$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid superpage alignment}
  \EndIf
  \State $\mathit{pa} \gets \mathit{as.physical} \lor
    (\mathit{pte.ppn} << \mathit{shift}) \lor
    (\mathit{va} \land \mathit{mask})$
  \State \Return $\mathit{pa}$
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\subsection{nested address translation}

the architecture supports nested address translation%
~\cite{popek1974virtualization} with per-domain
software-defined TLB miss handlers via a combination of the machine
extension and the domain extension. the capability extension is used to
limit control of this functionality to designated domains.
there is support for a global TLB miss handler implementing the
fixed page table formats defined by the architecture, as well as per-domain
software-defined TLB miss handlers.
\para
domains specify a machine default domain in \texttt{domroute[domain].mac},
the machine context for nested address translation.
when the domain and machine extensions are present,
\texttt{domcontrol.D} and \texttt{mcontrol.V} are set, and the
machine default domain is not self-mapped, then nested address
translation will be active. the machine default domain may be
self-mapped, in which case the domain context is a root machine context.

\subsubsection{software address translation}

when the machine extension is present, and \texttt{mcontrol.S} is set,
software address translation will be active. TLB miss faults will be
raised in the machine default domain defined in \texttt{domroute[domain].mac},
the machine context for address translation.
\para
the following registers are read or written by the machine context TLB miss handler.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \texttt{sptr} contains input page table root address and ASID.
\item \texttt{mtlbkey} contains the input faulting address used as input to the miss handler.
\item \texttt{mtlbscope} contains the output page size, domain number, and ASID.
\item \texttt{mtlbent} contains the output physical address, permission and metadata bits.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{nested software address translation}

when the domain and machine extensions are present,
\texttt{domcontrol.D} and \texttt{mcontrol.S} are set, and the
machine default domain is not self-mapped, then nested software address
translation is active and machine trap handler addresses are
\emph{domain physical addresses}. when nested software address
translation is active, the implementation runs the TLB miss handler for
the handler address of the faulting domain in its machine default domain.
this is to ensure handler address TLB entries are present before
recursively invoking the handler.
the TLB miss handler for a subordinate domain can be invoked in its
default context using \texttt{maccall}.
\para
the following handler vectors are used by nested machine context TLB miss handlers.
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \texttt{machpc} contains per domain machine handler program counter.
\item \texttt{machib} contains per domain machine handler immediate base.
\end{itemize}

\newpage
\section{Security model}

the domain and capability extensions define the principal isolation
mechanism for the architecture, in addition to intrinsic user
and system role isolation with address space prefixes.

\subsection{extensions}

the domain extension provides isolated contexts with role-based trap
routing and context switching.
\iffalse
the capability extension provides role-based instruction execution
and memory access permissions based on page table entry colors.
\else
the capability extension provides capability role isolation and memory
access permissions based on page table entry colors.
\fi
the machine extension provides access to memory type configuration,
first-level and second-level software-defined address translation handlers.
access to privileged system, domain, capability, and machine registers
requires the corresponding role.
the message role is required to receive inter-domain message interrupts.

\subsection{roles}

the architecture defines roles that form the isolation and privilege
model across execution, memory, and communication domains.
\para
\textbf{user role:}
the user role provides the least-privileged context for
user-level applications. it protects the privileged roles from untrusted
software by restricting access to user-level instructions and memory
in the user address space.
\para
\textbf{system role:}
the system role provides the privileged context for trusted systems software.
it provides access to system instructions, fault handlers for the debugger,
illegal instruction, alignment, page, and memory access faults, and memory
in the system address space.
\para
\textbf{domain role:}
the domain role provides the privileged context to configure isolated domain
execution contexts, including configuration of trap routing registers for
role-based exception delivery.
it protects software components from one another via domain boundaries.
\para
\textbf{capability role:}
the capability role provides the privileged context to associate page table
\iffalse
colors with memory access permissions and roles, to enable masking of memory
access permissions and restriction of executable pages to specific roles.
\else
colors with memory access permissions, to enable masking of memory
access permissions.
\fi
\para
\textbf{machine role:}
the machine role provides the privileged context to configure the memory
type registers, to control address translation, and to run software-defined
TLB miss handlers for custom first-level and second-level address translation.
\para
\textbf{message role:}
the message role provides the privileged context to process inter-domain
messages, which include locally-scoped synchronous message instructions,
and globally-scoped asynchronous message exceptions, such as timers and
message-signaled interrupts.

\newpage
\subsection{permissions}

the capability extension associates page table colors with additional
permission masks, which are combined with page table permissions during
memory access checks. the capability permission registers give control
over read and write permissions for page table colors.

\subsection{address spaces}
the architecture derives user and system roles from unary address prefixes,
enforcing address space isolation between user and system software during
execution.
implicit address space role checking is applied to every memory access and
instruction fetch, independently of page table permissions, providing a
defence-in-depth layer beneath the capability system.
\para
implicit address space role checking is used in the address translation
in place of a \emph{user} bit in page table entries.
user software is prevented from accessing the system address space.
system software is prevented from accessing or executing the user
address space via the \emph{user address space access disable} and
\emph{user address space execute disable} system control bits.

\subsection{exceptions}

the domain and capability extensions enable capability role and permission
exceptions, which are synchronous capability faults raised
when instruction execution violates a capability role check or a memory
access violates a capability permission check.
\para
capability role and permission exceptions are distinct from illegal
instruction and page fault exceptions, in that they reflect violations
of capability-level policy as opposed to a system exception.
capability faults are delivered through the domain extension
trap routing mechanism, allowing each domain to configure an independent
capability fault handler.
\para
the following table describes the architectural exception categories:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{exception} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
system     & illegal instruction, breakpoint, alignment, page, memory access fault \\
domain     & trap routing and inter-domain boundary violations \\
capability & capability role and permission policy violations \\
machine    & address translation and machine access violations \\
message    & inter-domain message delivery, timer events, or interrupt pins \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph exception categories}
\label{tab:glyph-exception-categories}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Virtual machine}

the architecture defines a virtual machine model with a system memory
map, power-on and reset behavior, firmware interface, hardware
description models, hypervisor interface, and virtual devices for I/O.

\subsection{system memory map}

the following table shows fixed regions of the system memory map.
the top 64~KiB of the boot firmware is mapped into the first 64~KiB of
memory starting at address zero. this is so that the reset vector
can point to a constant low address irrespective of the firmware location.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|r|r|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{start address} & \thead{end address} & \thead{size} & \thead{description}& \thead{type} \\
\hline
\texttt{0000} & \texttt{FFFF} & 64~KiB & boot firmware shadow & WB\footnotemark[1] \\
\texttt{C000\_0000\_0000} & \texttt{DFFF\_FFFF\_FFFF} & 32~TiB & physical memory & WB\footnotemark[1] \\
\texttt{E000\_0000\_0000} & \texttt{EFFF\_FFFF\_FFFF} & 16~TiB & device memory & WB,WC,WT,UC\footnotemark[1] \\
\texttt{EFFF\_FF00\_0000} & \texttt{EFFF\_FFFF\_FFFF} & 16~MiB & boot firmware & WB\footnotemark[1] \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph 48-bit address space memory map}
\label{tab:glyph-memory-map}
\end{table}

\footnotetext[1]{\emph{memory types}: WB = write-back, WC = write-combine, WT = write-through, UC = uncached}

\subsection{power-on and reset}

during power-on and reset, read-write fields of all system registers are
zeroed, unless otherwise specified, then the program counter and immediate
base register are set to the shadow reset vector addresses. cold reset
zeros the predicate register, plus general and vector registers. warm reset
preserves the predicate register, plus general and vector registers.
\para
all processors must start at the reset vector after cold or warm reset.
an implementation-defined mechanism is used to ensure system-wide unique
addresses are assigned to all processors.
if the domain and machine extensions are both present, it is up to the
firmware to ensure unique addresses are assigned to the \texttt{macaddr}
vector for the active domain.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|r|r|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{program counter} & \thead{immediate base} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{FF80} & \texttt{FFC0} & shadow reset vector addresses \\
\texttt{EFFF\_FFFF\_FF80} & \texttt{EFFF\_FFFF\_FFC0} & firmware reset vector addresses \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph 48-bit address space reset vector}
\label{tab:glyph-reset-vector}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{firmware interface}

the base virtual machine model provides early boot interfaces to bootloaders
and operating systems using the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI).
the following minimal set of UEFI protocols are required in the base
virtual machine model.
\para
the firmware is responsible for setting up boot page tables with a
self-map of physical memory, enabling page-based virtual address
translation, providing the hardware description in device tree format,
initializing devices required for loading the bootloader, then loading
and executing the bootloader by calling into \texttt{EfiMain}.
the firmware runs with the \emph{system} role.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{EFI\_LOADED\_IMAGE\_PROTOCOL}        & image context and load parameters \\
\texttt{EFI\_DEVICE\_PATH\_PROTOCOL}         & device and image location encoding \\
\texttt{EFI\_BLOCK\_IO\_PROTOCOL}            & block device access to boot media \\
\texttt{EFI\_SERIAL\_IO\_PROTOCOL}           & serial device access to console \\
\texttt{EFI\_SIMPLE\_FILE\_SYSTEM\_PROTOCOL} & filesystem access for boot media \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine UEFI protocols}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-uefi-protocols}
\end{table}

\subsection{hardware description}

the base virtual machine model uses device tree or ACPI, and optionally
SMBIOS, to describe the hardware.
the description provides a detailed map of memory, devices, bus hierarchies,
memory-mapped I/O regions, interrupt routing and other device-specific
configuration properties to firmware and system software.
device tree, ACPI and optional SMBIOS data is made available to bootloaders
and system software via EFI configuration tables.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{symbol} & \thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\textbf{DTS} & device tree source & the hardware description format \\
\textbf{DTB} & device tree binary & the compiled binary object \\
\textbf{FDT} & flattened device tree & the runtime device tree format \\
\textbf{AML} & ACPI machine language & the ACPI bytecode description format \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph hardware description formats}
\label{tab:glyph-hardware-description-formats}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{symbol} & \thead{GUID} \\
\hline
\texttt{EFI\_FDT\_TABLE\_GUID} & \texttt{B1B621D5-F19C-41A5-830B-D9152C69AAE0} \\
\texttt{EFI\_ACPI2\_TABLE\_GUID} & \texttt{8868E871-E4F1-11D3-BC22-0080C73C8881} \\
\texttt{EFI\_SMBIOS3\_TABLE\_GUID} & \texttt{F2FD1544-9794-4A2C-992E-E5BBCF20E394} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph EFI hardware description table GUIDs}
\label{tab:efi-fdt-table-guid}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{hypervisor interface}

the hypervisor interface virtualizes the architectural mechanisms used to
implement virtual machines.
the \emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine}, and \emph{message}
roles control domain switching, trap routing, cache coherency, memory
permissions, and software-defined address translation. these mechanisms are
exposed to systems software through a unified interface.
\para
the hypervisor is responsible for configuring context switching through the
domain trap routing registers, configuring capability-based page-table color
permissions through the capability permission registers, and configuring
cache coherency and address translation through the machine memory registers.
it also initializes, saves, and restores privileged registers during context
switching, including starting, suspending, and resuming an isolated context.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{symbol} & \thead{role} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{usr} & user       & execution of user-level programs \\
\texttt{sys} & system     & operating system software \\
\texttt{dom} & domain     & domain scheduling and routing \\
\texttt{cap} & capability & capabilities and permissions \\
\texttt{mac} & machine    & software-defined memory management \\
\texttt{msg} & message    & globally-scoped inter-domain messages \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine roles}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-roles}
\end{table}

\subsection{virtual devices}

the base virtual machine model recommends OASIS Virtual I/O (VirtIO) for
virtual devices. the following VirtIO device models are recommended for
inclusion in the base model.
\para
the virtual devices provide the interface between the guest operating
system and virtualized I/O devices. each device implements a VirtIO protocol
and exposes standard operations such as configuration, data transfer, and
interrupt signaling. the firmware and system software may use VirtIO
device drivers for platform-independent I/O in virtual environments.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{virtio-net}     & network device \\
\texttt{virtio-block}   & block device \\
\texttt{virtio-console} & console device \\
\texttt{virtio-rng}     & entropy source \\
\texttt{virtio-9p}      & 9p filesystem \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine virtual devices}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-virtual-devices}
\end{table}

\newcommand{\insncell}[6]{%
  % #1=xl #2=xr #3=left-bit-label #4=right-bit-label #5=header #6=value
  \fill[black!10](#1,0)rectangle(#2,1);
  \draw[line width=0.2mm](#1,0) rectangle(#2,1);
  \draw[line width=0.2mm](#1,-1)rectangle(#2,0);
  \node[font=\tiny,anchor=base]at({#1+0.5},1.2){\textsf{#3}};
  \node[font=\tiny,anchor=base]at({#2-0.5},1.2){\textsf{#4}};
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\midx}{(#1+#2)/2}%
  \node[font=\small,anchor=base]at(\midx,0.25){\textsf{#5}};
  \node[font=\small,anchor=base]at(\midx,-0.8){\textbf{#6}};
}

\newcommand{\insnopsize}[2]{%
  \insncell{9}{14}{6}{2}{opcode}{#1}%
  \insncell{14}{16}{1}{0}{size}{00}%
  \node[font=\small,anchor=base west]at(17.0,-0.8){$#2$};
}

\newcommand{\insnimmnine}[3]{
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.5]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]
\insncell{0}{9}{15}{7}{imm}{#1}
\insnopsize{#2}{#3}
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
}

\newcommand{\insrcimmsix}[4]{
