\insncell{3}{9}{12}{7}{imm}{#2}
\insnopsize{#3}{#4}
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
}

\newcommand{\insrcrbimmthree}[5]{
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.5]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]
\insncell{0}{3}{15}{13}{rc}{#1}
\insncell{3}{6}{12}{10}{rb}{#2}
\insncell{6}{9}{9}{7}{imm}{#3}
\insnopsize{#4}{#5}
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
}

\newcommand{\insrcrbrc}[5]{
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.5]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]
\insncell{0}{3}{15}{13}{rc}{#1}
\insncell{3}{6}{12}{10}{rb}{#2}
\insncell{6}{9}{9}{7}{ra}{#3}
\insnopsize{#4}{#5}
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
}

\section{Instruction listing --- 16-bit}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{break}
\insnimmnine{uimm}{00000}{\textbf{break}\: \mathit{uimm9}}
\\
the \emph{break} instruction causes a debugger trap.
\emph{program counter} and trap cause are saved to privileged
registers for the operating system to dispatch to a debugger
and the \emph{program counter} is set to a trap vector address.

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{j}
\insnimmnine{simm}{00001}{\textbf{j}\: \mathit{simm9} \times 2}
\\
the \emph{j} or \emph{jump} instruction is an unconditional branch instruction
that adds a relative immediate address to the \emph{program counter}. the
resulting \emph{program counter} address is \emph{$[pc + simm9 \times 2]$}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    pc = pc + simm9 * 2
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{b}
\insnimmnine{simm}{00010}{\textbf{b}\: \mathit{simm9} \times 2}
\\
the \emph{b} or \emph{branch} instruction is a conditional branch instruction
that adds a relative immediate address to the \emph{program counter}. if the
\emph{flag} register has been set by a compare instruction, the resulting
\emph{program counter} address is \emph{$[pc + simm9 \times 2]$},
otherwise the \emph{program counter} is advanced normally.
\begin{Verbatim}
    if flag:
        pc = pc + simm9 * 2
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{ibj}
\insnimmnine{simm}{00011}{\textbf{ibj}\: \mathit{simm9} \times 64}
\\
the \emph{ibj} or \emph{immediate-block-jump} instruction adds a
64-bit relative address to the \emph{immediate base} register.
the resulting \emph{immediate base} address is \emph{$[ib + simm9 \times 64]$}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    ib = ib + simm9 * 64
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{link}
\insrcimmsix{fun}{uimm}{00100}{\textbf{link.i64}\: \mathit{fun3}, \mathit{ib64}(\mathit{uimm6})}
\\
the \emph{link} instruction loads a \emph{i32x2} relative address
vector addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 8]$}. it adds the relative
address vector to \emph{(pc,ib)}, conditionally subtracts the source link
register \emph{(r6 or r7)}, then conditionally adds the difference to the
destination link register \emph{(r6 or r7)}, depending on the value
of \emph{fun3}. the type of linkage in \emph{fun3} can be one of:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{value} & \thead{mnemonic} & \thead{description} &
\thead{link-type} & \thead{dst-link} & \thead{src-link} \\
\hline
0     & j      & jump               & jump & -  & -  \\
1     & j      & jump               & jump & -  & -  \\
2     & jal    & jump-and-link      & call & r6 & -  \\
3     & jal    & jump-and-link      & call & r7 & -  \\
4     & jtl    & jump-to-link       & ret  & -  & r6 \\
5     & jtl    & jump-to-link       & ret  & -  & r7 \\
6     & jala   & jump-and-link-add  & tail & r6 & r6 \\
7     & jala   & jump-and-link-add  & tail & r7 & r7 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Link instruction functions}
\label{tab:link-instruction-functions}
\end{table}

\vspace{-1em}

\begin{Verbatim}
    lr = 0b110 + (fun3 & 1)
    (dpc,dib) = const-mem<i32x2>[ib + uimm6 * 8]
    (lpc,lib) = (0,0)
    if fun3 == jala:
        (dpc,dib) += auth-decrypt<i32x2>(reg[lr])
    if fun3 == jtl:
        (lpc,lib) += auth-decrypt<i32x2>(reg[lr])
    pc = pc + dpc - lpc
    ib = ib + dib - lib
    if fun3 == jal or fun3 == jala:
        reg[lr] = auth-encrypt<i32x2>((dpc,dib))
\end{Verbatim}

\par\noindent
the \emph{auth-encrypt} and \emph{auth-decrypt} functions are placeholders
for authenticated encryption, and decryption of relative address vectors.
authentication failures should generate a trap for the operating system to
dispatch to a control flow integrity trap handler.

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{movd}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{00101}{\textbf{movd.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})}
\\
the \emph{movd} or \emph{move-dword-immediate-block} instruction loads a
32-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$}, which it
sign-extends to 64-bits, then saves to the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{movq}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{00110}{\textbf{movq.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib64}(\mathit{uimm6})}
\\
the \emph{movq} or \emph{move-qword-immediate-block} instruction loads a
64-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 8]$} then saves
it to the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = const-mem<i64>[ib + uimm6 * 8]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{movi}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{simm}{00111}{\textbf{movi.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{simm6}}
\\
the \emph{movi} or \emph{move-immediate} instruction sign-extends the
immediate value in \emph{simm6} then saves the result in the \emph{rc}
register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = simm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{addi}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{simm}{01000}{\textbf{addi.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{simm6}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{addi} or \emph{add-immediate} instruction sign-extends the
immediate value in \emph{simm6} then adds it to the \emph{rc} register,
and carry is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rc] + simm6
    flag = result < reg[rc]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{srli}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01001}{\textbf{srli.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{uimm6}}
\\
the \emph{srli} or \emph{shift-right-logical-immediate} instruction
performs a logical right shift by \emph{uimm6} bits of the value in
the \emph{rc} register. zeros are copied into the left most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<u64>[rc] >> uimm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{srai}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01010}{\textbf{srai.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{uimm6}}
\\
the \emph{srai} or \emph{shift-right-arithmetic-immediate} instruction
performs an arithmetic right shift by \emph{uimm6} bits of the value in
the \emph{rc} register. the sign is copied into the left most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<i64>[rc] >> uimm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{slli}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01011}{\textbf{slli.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{uimm6}}
\\
the \emph{slli} or \emph{shift-left-logical-immediate} instruction
performs a logical left shift by \emph{uimm6} bits of the value in
the \emph{rc} register. zeros are copied into the right most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rc] << uimm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{addd}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01100}{\textbf{addd.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6}); \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{addd} or \emph{add-half-immediate-block} instruction loads
a 32-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$}, which it
sign-extends to 64-bits, then adds to the \emph{rc} register,
and carry is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rc] + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]
    flag = result < reg[rc]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{leapc}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01101}{\textbf{lea.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})(\mathit{pc})}
\\
the \emph{lea-pc} instruction loads a 32-bit constant addressed by
\emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$}, which it sign-extends to 64-bits, adds it to
the \emph{program counter}, and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = pc + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{loadpc}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01110}{\textbf{load.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})(\mathit{pc})}
\\
the \emph{load-pc} instruction loads a 32-bit constant addressed by
\emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$} which it sign-extends to 64-bit, adds it to
the \emph{program counter} to form an address, then loads a 64-bit value
from memory at that address and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = mem<i64>[pc + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{storepc}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01111}{\textbf{store.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})(\mathit{pc})}
\\
the \emph{store-pc} instruction loads a 32-bit constant addressed by
\emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$} which it sign-extends to 64-bit, adds it to the
\emph{program counter} to form an address, then stores to memory at that
address a 64-bit value from the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    mem<i64>[pc + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]] = reg[rc]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{load}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{uimm}{10000}{\textbf{load.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, (\mathit{uimm3} \times 8)(\mathit{rb})}
\\
the \emph{load} instruction computes the address \emph{$[rb + uimm3 \times 8]$}
then loads a 64-bit value from memory at that address and saves the result in
the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = mem<i64>[reg[rb] + uimm3 * 8]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{store}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{uimm}{10001}{\textbf{store.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, (\mathit{uimm3} \times 8)(\mathit{rb})}
\\
the \emph{store} instruction computes the address \emph{$[rb + uimm3 \times 8]$}
then stores a 64-bit value to memory at that address containing a 64-bit value
from the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    mem<i64>[reg[rb] + uimm3 * 8] = reg[rc]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{compare}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{fun}{10010}{\textbf{cmp.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{fun3}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{compare} instruction performs a comparison between the value in
\emph{rb} and \emph{rc} then saves the result in the \emph{flag} register.
the \emph{compare} opcode is also used to perform conditional move whereby
the \emph{rb} register is copied into the \emph{rc} if the \emph{flag}
register is set. the type of comparison in \emph{fun3} can be one of:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{value} & \thead{mnemonic}& \thead{description} \\
\hline
0     & \emph{lt}    & \emph{less than (signed)}          \\
1     & \emph{ge}    & \emph{greather or equal (signed)}  \\
2     & \emph{eq}    & \emph{equal}                       \\
3     & \emph{ne}    & \emph{not equal}                   \\
4     & \emph{ltu}   & \emph{less than (unsigned)}        \\
5     & \emph{geu}   & \emph{greater or equal (unsigned)} \\
6     & \emph{cmov}  & \emph{conditional move}            \\
7     & \emph{ncmov} & \emph{negated conditional move}    \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Compare instruction functions}
\label{tab:compare-instruction-functions}
\end{table}

\begin{Verbatim}
    match fun3
    | lt    -> flag = reg<i64>[rc] <  reg<i64>[rb]
    | ge    -> flag = reg<i64>[rc] >= reg<i64>[rb]
    | eq    -> flag =      reg[rc] =       reg[rb]
    | ne    -> flag =      reg[rc] !=      reg[rb]
    | ltu   -> flag = reg<u64>[rc] <  reg<u64>[rb]
    | geu   -> flag = reg<u64>[rc] >= reg<u64>[rb]
    | cmov  -> if flag:
                   reg[rc] = reg[rb]
    | ncmov -> if not flag:
                   reg[rc] = reg[rb]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{logic}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{fun}{10011}{\textbf{logic.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{fun3}}
\\
the \emph{logic} instruction performs a logic operation on the value
in the \emph{rb} register then stores the result in the \emph{rc}
register. the type of logic operations in \emph{fun3} can be one of:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{value} & \thead{mnemonic}& \thead{description} \\
\hline
0     & \emph{mov}   & \emph{move}                 \\
1     & \emph{not}   & \emph{logical not}          \\
2     & \emph{neg}   & \emph{negate}               \\
3     & \emph{bswap} & \emph{byte swap}            \\
4     & \emph{ctz}   & \emph{count trailing zeros} \\
5     & \emph{clz}   & \emph{count leading zeros}  \\
6     & \emph{ctpop} & \emph{count population}     \\
7     & \emph{sext}  & \emph{sign extend}          \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Logic instruction functions}
\label{tab:logic-instruction-functions}
\end{table}

\begin{Verbatim}
    match fun3
    | mov   -> reg[rc] = reg[rb]
    | not   -> reg[rc] = ~reg[rb]
    | neg   -> reg[rc] = -reg[rb]
    | bswap -> reg[rc] = bswap(reg[rb])
    | ctz   -> reg[rc] = ctz(reg[rb])
    | clz   -> reg[rc] = clz(reg[rb])
    | ctpop -> reg[rc] = ctpop(reg[rb])
    | sext  -> reg[rc] = sext(reg[rb])
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{pin}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10100}{\textbf{pin.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{pin} or \emph{pack-indirect} instruction packs two absolute
addresses as an \emph{i32x2} \emph{(pc,ib)} relative address vector.
the register \emph{ra} is subtracted from the \emph{program counter},
and the register \emph{rb} is subtracted from the \emph{immediate base}
register, and the results are packed into an \emph{i32x2} relative address
vector and saved to the register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    lpc   = int<i32>(pc - reg[ra])
    lib   = int<i32>(ib - reg[rb])
    lval  = (lpc,lib)
    reg[rc] = auth-encrypt(lval)
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{and}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10101}{\textbf{and.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{and} instruction performs a \emph{logical-and} of the register
\emph{rb} and the register \emph{ra} and saves the result in the
register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] & reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{or}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10110}{\textbf{or.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{or} instruction performs a \emph{logical-or} of the register
\emph{rb} and the register \emph{ra} and saves the result in the
register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] | reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{xor}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10111}{\textbf{xor.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{xor} instruction performs a \emph{logical-exclusive-or}
of the register \emph{rb} and the register \emph{ra} and saves the
result in the register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] ^ reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{add}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11000}{\textbf{add.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{add} instruction adds the \emph{rb} register to
the \emph{ra} register and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register,
and carry is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rb] + reg[ra]
    flag = result < reg[rb]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{srl}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11001}{\textbf{srl.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{srl} or \emph{shift-right-logical} instruction performs a
logical right shift of the value in the \emph{rb} register by the
number of bits in register \emph{ra[5:0]} then saves the result in the
\emph{rc} register.
zeros are copied into the right most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<u64>[rb] >> (reg[ra] & 63)
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{sra}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11010}{\textbf{sra.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{sra} or \emph{shift-right-arithmetic} instruction performs an
arithmetic right shift of the value in the \emph{rb} register by the
number of bits in register \emph{ra[5:0]} then saves the result in the
\emph{rc} register. sign is copied into the right most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<i64>[rb] >> (reg[ra] & 63)
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{sll}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11011}{\textbf{sll.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{sll} or \emph{shift-left-logical} instruction performs a
logical left shift of the value in the \emph{rb} register by the
number of bits in register \emph{ra[5:0]} then saves the result in the
\emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] << (reg[ra] & 63)
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{sub}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11100}{\textbf{sub.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{sub} instruction subtracts the \emph{ra} register from
the \emph{rb} register and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register,
and carry \emph{not borrow} is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\textbf{note:} this differs from borrow semantics; flag is \emph{true carry}
and can be used with \emph{adc} or \emph{sbc} instructions without inversion.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rb] - reg[ra]
    flag = reg[rb] >= reg[ra]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{mul}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11101}{\textbf{mul.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{mul} instruction performs signed multiplication of the
\emph{rb} register with the \emph{ra} register and saves the
result in the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] * reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{div}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11110}{\textbf{div.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{div} instruction performs signed division of the
\emph{rb} register by the \emph{ra} register and saves the
result in the \emph{rc} register. division by zero causes
the flag to be set and zero to be stored in the \emph{rc} register.
a subsequent branch can handle the division by zero.
\begin{Verbatim}
    flag = reg[ra] == 0
    if flag:
        reg[rc] = 0
    else:
        reg[rc] = reg[rb] / reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{illegal}
\insnimmnine{uimm}{11111}{\textbf{illegal}\: \mathit{uimm9}}
\\
the \emph{illegal} instruction causes an illegal instruction trap.
\emph{program counter} and trap cause are saved to privileged registers
for the operating system to dispatch to an illegal instruction handler
and the \emph{program counter} is set to a trap vector address.

\addtocontents{toc}{\protect\clearpage}
\chapter{Assembly}
\label{sec:assembly-reference}

\section{Introduction}

the assembly language reference begins with an introduction to
assembler and linker concepts and terminology, followed by sections
describing the assembler directives, and pseudo-instruction aliases.

\section{Concepts}
this section covers the concepts required to understand assembling and
linking executable code from source files. the terminology used in this
section is generally applicable to ELF, PE/COFF, and Mach-O formats, with
the exception that all code is position-independent.

\subsection{assembly}
assembly files contain assembly language directives, macros and
instructions describing program code and data. they can be handwritten
or emitted by a compiler. an assembly file is the input file to the
assembler and the output from the assembler is an object file.

\subsection{object}
object files contain compiled position-independent object code and data with
relocations. an object file cannot be run; rather it is used as input
to the archiver or linker in archiving or linking steps that combine them
to produce an archive or an executable file or library.

\subsection{archive}
archive files contain a collection of compiled position-independent object
files with relocations and are sometimes referred to as static libraries.
archive files use a simple format that concatenates a set of object
files with an index listing the objects contained in the archive.
thin archives contain the index listing the object files without
the associated data.

\subsection{executable}
executable files contain compiled position-independent object code and
data that has been linked together by a linker in a linking step
using multiple object files, archive files, and libraries as input.
executable files can be statically or dynamically linked. dynamically
linked executables express dependencies on libraries which are
loaded at runtime.

\subsection{library}
library files are runtime-loadable archive files containing position-%
independent object code and data, together with a symbol table and metadata.
they are produced by a linker from multiple object files, archive files, and
libraries as input. libraries can be loaded by a runtime loader, and they
express dependencies on other libraries which need to be loaded at runtime.

\subsection{section}
a section is a name for a region of code or data in an object file,
executable file or library file. the files can be made up of
multiple sections where each section corresponds to one specific type of
executable code or data. this list contains the most common types:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \texttt{.text} is a read-only section containing executable code
\item \texttt{.const} is a read-only section containing immediate blocks
\item \texttt{.data} is a read-write section containing global or static variables
\item \texttt{.rodata} is a read-only section containing read-only variables
\item \texttt{.bss} is a read-write section containing uninitialized data
\end{itemize}

\subsection{segment}
segments are loadable regions of code or data in an executable or shared
library. segments describe virtual addresses, file offsets and memory access
permissions for mapped sections. in ELF, a segment can map to one or more
sections. in PE/COFF sections are mapped directly. in Mach-O, sections are
contained within a small set of specific segment types.

\subsection{symbol}
symbols are entries in a metadata table that contain a name with a
mapping to an address. symbols are present in object files and dynamic
symbols are present in libraries and executables. they can be
referred to in relocation entries and debugging metadata.

\subsection{relocation}
relocations are metadata table entries used to update relocatable addresses
during linking. relocations contain a type, a file offset pointing to text
or data, and a pointer to a symbol name whose address needs to be updated
during the linking step. relocations can be present in object files and
dynamic relocations are present in libraries and executables.

\subsection{linking}
linking is the process of combining multiple relocatable object
files by merging and aligning sections, resolving symbol references across
files, assigning final symbol addresses, applying relocation fix-ups using
relocation entries, and adding debug metadata.

\newpage
\section{Directives}
the assembler implements a number of directives that control the assembly
of instructions into object files. these directives are based on the
AT\&T System V assembler~\cite{att-svr4-asm} and the GNU assembler%
~\cite{gnu-as-manual}, with some additions. they provide the ability to
include arbitrary data, align data, export symbols, switch sections,
define constants, and emit metadata.
\para
the following table lists \arch{} assembler directives:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Directive} & \thead{Arguments} & \thead{Description} \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{Data directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.byte}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 8-bit comma separated words   \\
\texttt{.short}  & \emph{expression-list}    & 16-bit comma separated words  \\
\texttt{.long}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 32-bit comma separated words  \\
\texttt{.quad}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 64-bit comma separated words  \\
\texttt{.octa}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 128-bit comma separated words \\
\texttt{.string} & \emph{“string”}           & emit string                   \\
\texttt{.zero}   & \emph{integer}            & emit zeroes                   \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{Alignment directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.align}  & \emph{pow2 [,pad\_val=0] [,max]} & align to power of 2    \\
\texttt{.balign} & \emph{bytes [,pad\_val=0]}       & byte align             \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{Symbol directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.globl}  & \emph{symbol\_name,const\_name}  & emit symbol \emph{(global scope)}  \\
\texttt{.local}  & \emph{symbol\_name,const\_name}  & emit symbol \emph{(local scope)}   \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{Section directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.text}    &         & emit \texttt{.text} section or make current    \\
\texttt{.const}   &         & emit \texttt{.const} section or make current   \\
\texttt{.data}    &         & emit \texttt{.data} section or make current    \\
\texttt{.rodata}  &         & emit \texttt{.rodata} section or make current  \\
\texttt{.bss}     &         & emit \texttt{.bss} section or make current     \\
\texttt{.common}  & \emph{symbol\_name,size,align} & emit common object to
\texttt{.bss} section \\
\texttt{.section} & \emph{section\_name}    & emit section (default
\texttt{.text}) or make current \\
\hline
\rowcolor{tscol}
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\tsub{Miscellaneous directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.equ}    & \emph{name, value}       & constant definition            \\
\texttt{.file}   & \emph{“filename”}        & emit filename symbol           \\
\texttt{.ident}  & \emph{“string”}          & emit identification string     \\
\texttt{.size}   & \emph{symbol,symbol}     & emit symbol size               \\
\texttt{.type}   & \emph{symbol,@function}  & emit symbol type               \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Assembler directives}
\label{tab:assembler-directives}
\end{table}

\section{Pseudo-instructions}

the assembler implements a number of convenience pseudo-instruction aliases
that are formed from regular instructions, but have implicit or deduced arguments.
\para
the following table lists \arch{} assembler pseudo-instruction aliases:

\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{Pseudo-instruction} & \thead{Expansion} & \thead{Description}   \\
\hline

\newpage
\section{Application binary interface}

the application binary interface is the set of specifications that
determine the calling conventions, object, archive, debug, and
executable file formats, static and dynamic linking, thread local
storage, and program loading behavior for the architecture.
the calling convention covers register classes and preservation
rules, such as argument and return value registers, stack pointer,
frame pointer, return address, and temporary registers, caller-save
or callee-save behavior, parameter-passing register assignment rules,
and stack alignment.
\para
\S\ref{sec:assembly-reference} covers the assembler and linker
conventions.
\S\ref{sec:calling-convention-general-min} defines the calling convention
for the \emph{general-min} embedded architectural profile targeting
the 16-bit instruction subset with support for 8 general registers.
the application binary interfaces for the other architectural profiles:
\emph{general-max}, \emph{vector-min}, and \emph{vector-max}, are defined
in processor supplement documents for their respective binary formats,
including the following:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{format} & \thead{ABI} & \thead{description} \\
\hline
\textbf{PE}     & \textsf{glyph-pe}    & Portable Executable Format \\
\textbf{ELF}    & \textsf{glyph-elf}   & Executable and Linkable Format \\
\textbf{Mach-O} & \textsf{glyph-macho} & Mach Object Format \\
\textbf{DWARF}  & \textsf{glyph-dwarf} & Debugging Information Format \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph binary formats and ABIs}
\label{tab:glyph-binary-formats-and-abis}
\end{table}

\section{Calling conventions}

it is recommended that all code targeting the architecture is
position-independent.
the stack pointer must be in register \texttt{r0}, and the return
address must be in register \texttt{r7}.
the stack pointer should be aligned to 64-bytes on function entry,
to support 512-bit aligned vector memory instructions using the
\emph{vector-min} and \emph{vector-max} architectural profiles.
the \emph{immediate base} register must point to a valid immediate
block on function entry, and be restored to that value in the
\emph{epilogue} to support relative link address vectors.
split instruction and constant streams require functions to export two
labels, one in each of the \texttt{.text} and \texttt{.const} sections.
the remainder of the details are to be defined in the processor
supplement documents.

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{general-min} calling convention}
\label{sec:calling-convention-general-min}

the 16-bit instruction packet, while intended to be used in conjunction
with the larger opcodes, is designed as a complete subset, so there is
an ABI variant that targets a subset of the instruction set architecture
that only uses the 16-bit opcodes.
\para
the register assignment for the 16-bit subset was chosen with this rationale:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item  2 blocks of 4 contiguous non-volatile \emph{callee-save} and volatile
\emph{caller-save} registers.
\item  3 special registers, 2 argument registers, 1 temporary register,
and 3 save registers.
\item  3 save registers to avoid excessive spilling around function calls.
\item  1 temporary register to avoid spilling arguments to free a temporary.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the calling convention for the 16-bit subset is as follows:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \emph{immediate base} \texttt{ib} is set by \texttt{call} instructions
and must point to a valid immediate block on function entry. function
symbols are exported with two labels; one in the \texttt{.text} section, and
one in the \texttt{.const} section. \emph{immediate base} must be restored
to the entry value in the function \emph{epilogue} before it can be restored
by \texttt{ret}.
\item \emph{argument registers} \texttt{a0} and \texttt{a1} are used for the
first two arguments, and the remaining arguments are passed on the stack.
\emph{return value} is passed in \texttt{a0} and \texttt{a1},
\emph{temporary register} \texttt{t0} is a volatile register, and
\emph{frame pointer} (if enabled) uses \texttt{s0}. there are two more
non-volatile \emph{callee-save} registers, \texttt{s1} and \texttt{s2}.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following table outlines the 16-bit register allocation showing
register name alias, description, and non-volatile \emph{callee-save} or
volatile \emph{caller-save} status.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\rowcolors{2}{tbcol}{tbcol}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\rowcolor{thcol}
\thead{name} & \thead{alias} & \thead{description} & \thead{save} \\
\hline
r0    & sp    & stack pointer                               & callee \\
r1    & s0/fp & saved register 0 / frame pointer            & callee \\
r2    & s1    & saved register 1                            & callee \\
r3    & s2    & saved register 2                            & callee \\
r4    & a0    & argument register 0                         & caller \\
r5    & a1    & argument register 1                         & caller \\
r6    & t0    & temporary register 0                        & caller \\
r7    & ra    & return address / (pc,ib) link vector        & caller \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{16-bit register assignment}
\label{tab:register-allocation-16}
\end{table}

\chapter{Appendix}

\section{Opcode summary --- 16-bit}

\newcommand{\acell}[5]{%
  % #1=row #2=xl #3=xr #4=midx #5=label
  \draw[line width=0.2mm](#2,{0-#1})rectangle(#3,{1-#1});
  \node[font=\small,anchor=base]at(#4,{0.25-#1}){\textbf{#5}};
}
\newcommand{\ahcell}[5]{%
  % #1=row #2=xl #3=xr #4=midx #5=label
  \fill[black!10](#2,{0-#1})rectangle(#3,{1-#1});
  \draw[line width=0.2mm](#2,{0-#1})rectangle(#3,{1-#1});
  \node[font=\small,anchor=base]at(#4,{0.25-#1}){\textsf{#5}};
}
\newcommand{\ainsnimmnine}[4]{
  \acell{#1}{0}{9}{4.5}{#2}
  \acell{#1}{9}{14}{11.5}{#3}
  \acell{#1}{14}{16}{15.0}{00}
  \node[font=\small,anchor=base west]at(17.0,{0.25-#1}){$#4$};
